<div class="paper-meta"><p class="paper-authors">Hui Kuurila-Zhang<sup>1</sup>, Hui Wei<sup>1</sup>, Hanliang Xie<sup>1</sup>, Haoyu Chen<sup>1</sup>, Guoying Zhao<sup>1,2</sup></p><p><sup>1</sup>Center for Machine Vision and Signal Analysis (CMVS), University of Oulu<br><sup>2</sup>ELLIS Institute Finland</p><p>Under review at <em>Transactions on Machine Learning Research</em> (TMLR).</p></div>

<h2 id="abstract">Abstract</h2>

Adversarial examples are carefully designed inputs to deep learning systems that cause incorrect predictions. For nearly a decade, the field studied them almost exclusively through additive $\ell_p$-norm-bounded perturbations, an abstraction that correlates poorly with human perception. A growing body of research studies <em>Unrestricted Adversarial Examples</em> (UAEs), they are adversarial examples that are synthesized from scratch or altered through semantically meaningful changes to color, geometry, style, or content, constrained not by a norm budget in pixel space but by the requirement that the change preserve the true label under human judgment and remain plausible to a human observer. We take a UAE to be any adversarial input satisfying these two conditions that cannot be expressed as an $\ell_p$-bounded change in pixel space, and we use this criterion to include and exclude methods throughout.

This is the first survey dedicated to UAEs in image classification. Prior surveys either omit this attack type entirely or cover only a small part of the image, and none offer a taxonomy that spans it. We close that gap. We organize the field by generation mechanism into five categories: transformation-based, distance metric-based, physical parameter-based, style transfer and content editing-based, and generative model-based. The generation mechanism largely determines a UAE’s core characteristics: perceptual visibility, realism, and perturbation flexibility. Conventional taxonomies, usually based on attacker knowledge and operational domain, are inherited from the norm-bounded literature and miss this dependency. We also consolidate UAE evaluation practice. Because UAEs share no common perturbation budget, existing work evaluates attacks inconsistently across attack success rate and perceptual realism, making cross-paper comparison difficult. We organize these evaluation dimensions into a single conceptual framework as a reference for future work.

We identify several open challenges. One is an emerging connection to mechanistic interpretability: the study of what features neural networks represent and how they compute with them. Several UAE categories, particularly transformation-based and style- and content editing-based methods, alter a named visual property such as color, texture, lightness, or image content. Because the altered property is known, such UAEs provide semantically grounded interventions that can probe how vision models internally represent and process that property. We survey early work in this direction and propose new research directions. More broadly, we offer a perspective linking adversarial vulnerability to the question of what vision models have learned in their internal representations.

<h2 id="section-1">1 Introduction</h2>

Deep neural networks (DNNs) match or surpass human performance in computer vision tasks such as image classification <a class="cite" href="#ref-simonyan2015very">Simonyan &amp; Zisserman (2015)</a>; <a class="cite" href="#ref-he2016deep">He et al. (2016)</a>; <a class="cite" href="#ref-vit">Dosovitskiy et al. (2021)</a>. Yet these models are vulnerable: carefully crafted inputs, known as adversarial examples, are imperceptible to humans yet reliably fool them <a class="cite" href="#ref-szegedy2013intriguing">Szegedy et al. (2013)</a>. Most adversarial examples are constructed by applying small, $\ell_p$-norm-constrained perturbations to clean images <a class="cite" href="#ref-fgsm">Goodfellow et al. (2015)</a>; <a class="cite" href="#ref-momentum-iterative">Dong et al. (2018)</a>; <a class="cite" href="#ref-autoattack">Croce &amp; Hein (2020)</a>. Such attacks constrain the perturbation through a single scalar budget $\epsilon$ on its norm, which limits its magnitude and, depending on the norm, its spatial spread.<sup class="fn"><a href="#footnote-1" id="footnote-ref-1" aria-label="Footnote 1">1</a></sup> We refer to these as <em>norm-bounded adversarial examples</em>.

The $\ell_p$-norm, however, is a poor proxy for perceptual similarity. It does not align with how humans judge visual change <a class="cite" href="#ref-lpips">Zhang et al. (2018)</a>. This has motivated <em>Unrestricted Adversarial Examples (UAEs)</em> <a class="cite" href="#ref-uae_origin">Song et al. (2018)</a>; <a class="cite" href="#ref-uaebrown">Brown et al. (2018)</a>. These still fool the target model, but the perturbation is no longer bounded by any $\ell_p$ budget in pixel space. Instead, the modification is constrained only by two perceptual requirements: a human oracle still assigns the original label, and the image remains plausible. Figure <a href="#figure-1">1</a> shows examples of norm-bounded adversarial examples and UAEs. It illustrates how UAEs are perceptible but visually plausible.

Bottom (from left to right): transformation-based, style transfer and content editing-based, and generative model-based UAEs. Each type shows a few representative examples rather than the full range of its category. See Section <a href="#section-3">3</a> for the full taxonomy.</figcaption></figure>

Without the norm constraint, adversaries have considerably more freedom. Attacks can synthesize adversarial images from scratch <a class="cite" href="#ref-sd-nae">Lin et al. (2024a)</a>; <a class="cite" href="#ref-advdiff">Dai et al. (2024)</a>; <a class="cite" href="#ref-advdiffuser">Chen et al. (2023a)</a>; <a class="cite" href="#ref-uae_origin">Song et al. (2018)</a>; <a class="cite" href="#ref-genmodel-reference-GNAE">Zhao et al. (2018)</a>, perturb original images in latent space <a class="cite" href="#ref-diffattack">Chen et al. (2024</a>; <a class="cite" href="#ref-advdiffuser">2023a)</a>; <a class="cite" href="#ref-diffpgd">Xue et al. (2023)</a>; <a class="cite" href="#ref-content-editding-ACA">Chen et al. (2023b)</a>; <a class="cite" href="#ref-genmodel-diff-concept">Zhang et al. (2026)</a>, or apply semantically meaningful transformations to color, texture, lightness, or spatial composition <a class="cite" href="#ref-color-functional-attack-nips-2019">Laidlaw &amp; Feizi (2019)</a>; <a class="cite" href="#ref-color-first-one-cvprw-2018">Hosseini &amp; Poovendran (2018)</a>; <a class="cite" href="#ref-geometric-engstrom">Engstrom et al. (2019)</a>; <a class="cite" href="#ref-geometric-stadv">Xiao et al. (2018b)</a>; <a class="cite" href="#ref-style-augmentation-AdvMix">Gowal et al. (2020)</a>. This freedom exposes vulnerabilities that conventional defenses, designed for minor pixel-level perturbations, are not built to address.

Existing surveys on adversarial examples focus predominantly on norm-bounded examples, leaving UAEs systematically underexplored. We present the first dedicated survey on this class of adversarial examples, offering a structured overview for both newcomers and researchers.

<figure id="figure-2"><a class="figure-link" href="/blog/beyond-the-lp-ball/figure-2.webp"><img src="/blog/beyond-the-lp-ball/figure-2.webp" alt="Stacked bar chart of the 210 collected papers per year from 2017 to 2026, split into generative sub-types, non-generative methods, non-method works and out-of-scope works, with a line for the generative total." width="1846" height="1208" loading="lazy" decoding="async"></a><figcaption><strong>Figure 2:</strong> The 210 collected papers by year, from 2017 to August 2026. Methods are grouped more coarsely than in our taxonomy to highlight the shift between the two mechanism families. The generative branch is shown by its three sub-types at the base of each bar, while the remaining categories are pooled as <em>non-generative-model-based</em> and analyzed individually in Section <a href="#section-3">3</a>. Non-generative work peaks in 2020 and disappears after 2024, whereas generative work, shown by the black line, remains at or below six papers per year through 2022 before increasing to 12-16 papers annually from 2023 onward. <em>Non-method works</em> include defenses, benchmarks, and theory, while <em>outside survey scope</em> denotes excluded attack categories, namely physical-world and patch methods, other tasks and modalities, and vision-language targets. The 2026 bar is hatched to indicate a partial year.</figcaption></figure>

<strong>Scope:</strong> This survey focuses on UAEs for image classification in the digital domain. The boundary reflects the practice we observed across the literature. Most of the methods that explicitly frame themselves as unrestricted are developed and evaluated on digital image classification benchmarks. Physical adversarial examples, which are naturally unconstrained by pixel-space $\ell_p$-norm bounds, share conceptual overlap with the unrestricted paradigm but fall outside our primary scope and are surveyed in <a class="cite" href="#ref-wei2024physical">Wei et al. (2024)</a>. We assembled a collection of 210 papers published between 2017 and 2026, identified by keyword search across several terms this literature uses for the same construct and extended by backward and forward citation tracing. We organize influential studies within a unified framework rather than compiling an exhaustive catalog. Readers seeking broader coverage across adversarial paradigms are referred to prior surveys <a class="cite" href="#ref-generalsurvey01">Akhtar &amp; Mian (2018)</a>; <a class="cite" href="#ref-generalsurvey02">Akhtar et al. (2021)</a>.

<strong>Existing surveys and our contributions:</strong> Adversarial examples have been the subject of numerous surveys, yet the organizing principles these works adopt are poorly suited to UAEs. Most existing surveys categorize attacks according to the adversary’s knowledge of the victim model, distinguishing white-box, gray-box, and black-box settings that correspond to full, partial, and zero knowledge of the targeted victim model, or according to whether a specific class is induced, distinguishing targeted from untargeted attacks. These taxonomies do not distinguish different types of UAEs in a meaningful way. What most distinguishes one UAE method from another is not the adversary’s access to the victim model but the perturbation space in which the attack operates, and a taxonomy built around model knowledge therefore fails to capture the distinctions that matter for this class of attacks.

The chronology of prior surveys further explains the gap in coverage. The earliest survey <a class="cite" href="#ref-generalsurvey01">Akhtar &amp; Mian (2018)</a> predates the formal definition of UAEs introduced in <a class="cite" href="#ref-uae_origin">Song et al. (2018)</a>; <a class="cite" href="#ref-uaebrown">Brown et al. (2018)</a> and consequently does not engage with the concept at all. Later surveys <a class="cite" href="#ref-zhang2019adversarial">Zhang &amp; Li (2019)</a>; <a class="cite" href="#ref-serban2020adversarial">Serban et al. (2020)</a>, though contemporary with or subsequent to that definition, nonetheless omit UAEs entirely, reflecting how recently this attack paradigm had emerged at the time of their writing. Subsequent work treats it only in passing: <a class="cite" href="#ref-machado2021adversarial">Machado et al. (2021)</a> notes a handful of UAE methods as “visible perturbations” without deeper analysis, while <a class="cite" href="#ref-survey-gan-cai2021generative">Cai et al. (2021)</a>; <a class="cite" href="#ref-survey-gan-zhang2023">Zhang et al. (2023)</a> restrict their attention to GAN-based instances. Several recent surveys <a class="cite" href="#ref-generalsurvey02">Akhtar et al. (2021)</a>; <a class="cite" href="#ref-survey-zhang2025tenyears">Zhang et al. (2025)</a>; <a class="cite" href="#ref-survey-guo2025beyond">Guo et al. (2025)</a> have begun to treat UAEs as a distinct category, devoting dedicated sections to their discussion. Nonetheless, their coverage remains incomplete, and each is constrained by a single methodological perspective. <a class="cite" href="#ref-generalsurvey02">Akhtar et al. (2021)</a> concentrates on color- and attribute-manipulation methods, <a class="cite" href="#ref-survey-zhang2025tenyears">Zhang et al. (2025)</a> characterizes UAEs as generative-model-based adversarial examples, and <a class="cite" href="#ref-survey-guo2025beyond">Guo et al. (2025)</a> summarizes them as latent-space attacks. Across this body of work, UAEs are thus either omitted entirely or reduced to a fragment of their full scope, with the remaining categories left unaddressed. None of these surveys, moreover, connects adversarial examples to mechanistic interpretability; although <a class="cite" href="#ref-survey-interpret2023">Han et al. (2023)</a> examines adversarial examples through the lens of interpretability, it focuses on interpreting the adversarial examples themselves rather than employing them as an intervention for mechanistic interpretability. Table <a href="#table-1">1</a> summarizes this comparison. The rapid development of UAE methods in recent years makes these limitations more significant, as earlier surveys may not reflect the most recent developments. Figure <a href="#figure-2">2</a> makes this concrete. The volume of work has been broadly stable since 2020, but its composition has turned over almost completely. Non-generative methods peak in 2020 and disappear from the collection after 2024, while diffusion-based methods are absent before 2023 but become dominant afterward. A taxonomy organized by generation mechanism captures this shift directly. One organized by adversary knowledge would not capture it at all.

To address this gap, we present a comprehensive survey that covers the peer-reviewed and impactful UAE literature organized around the perturbation space. Our survey makes the following four contributions, none of which has been addressed by existing surveys:

1. <strong>First survey dedicated to UAEs.</strong> We treat UAEs as the central object of study rather than a sub-section within a broader adversarial-attack survey, which allows us to address methodological nuances at a depth that prior reviews cannot.
2. <strong>A taxonomy organized by generation mechanism.</strong> UAE research has grown without a unified taxonomy. The same attack paradigm appears under different names across papers, and no common benchmarking protocol exists, leaving the literature fragmented and difficult to compare. We therefore organize UAEs by their generation mechanism (Section <a href="#section-3">3</a>). This is the clearest discriminator among existing methods. Every work states it explicitly, so it does not depend on paper-specific assumptions such as attacker knowledge or operational domain. It also correlates strongly with a method’s perturbation flexibility, which dominates how visible the resulting perturbation may be. It is thus the most informative organizing principle for a class of attacks defined by the absence of a norm constraint.
3. <strong>UAE-specific evaluation metrics and benchmarking.</strong> Section <a href="#section-5">5</a> covers evaluation criteria tailored to the distinctive properties of UAEs: attack effectiveness, perceptual realism and semantic fidelity, and a notion of <em>perturbation flexibility</em>, along with benchmark datasets and a unified performance comparison in Section <a href="#section-6">6</a>.
4. <strong>Repositioning UAEs as structured interventions for mechanistic interpretability.</strong> Section <a href="#section-8">8</a> argues that UAEs, which mostly perturb human-interpretable properties (color, geometry, style, content) rather than imperceptible $\ell_p$ noise, can act as structured interventions for mechanistic interpretability (MI; the study of reverse-engineering the internal representations learned by neural networks).

<figure id="figure-3"><a class="figure-link" href="/blog/beyond-the-lp-ball/figure-3.webp"><img src="/blog/beyond-the-lp-ball/figure-3.webp" alt="Diagram of the survey structure: four parts (Foundations, Attack &amp; Defense, Evaluation, Outlook) with their sections, and a strip listing the five UAE generation mechanisms." width="2000" height="996" loading="lazy" decoding="async"></a><figcaption><strong>Figure 3:</strong> Structure of this survey. The four parts move from <em>what a UAE is</em> (Part I), to <em>how UAEs are generated and resisted</em> (Part II), to <em>how they should be measured</em> (Part III), to <em>where the field is going</em> (Part IV). The strip beneath expands the taxonomy of Section <a href="#section-3">3</a> into its five families.</figcaption></figure>

<strong>Structure:</strong> Figure <a href="#figure-3">3</a> shows the structure of the survey. Section <a href="#section-2">2</a> establishes foundational concepts and definitions. Section <a href="#section-3">3</a> presents our five-category taxonomy of UAE generation techniques. Section <a href="#section-4">4</a> reviews defenses against UAEs and explains why those built for norm-bounded adversarial examples do not transfer. Section <a href="#section-5">5</a> introduces a unified conceptual evaluation framework for UAEs, and Section <a href="#section-6">6</a> provides a performance comparison of the surveyed methods under that framework. Section <a href="#section-7">7</a> discusses open challenges and future directions. Section <a href="#section-8">8</a> develops the connection between UAEs and mechanistic interpretability, and Section <a href="#section-9">9</a> concludes.

<h2 id="section-2">2 Preliminaries</h2>

<h3 id="section-2-1">2.1 The Lp Norm</h3>

Adversarial perturbation is usually defined with a norm. A norm measures the size of the perturbation added to an image. Let $\delta \in \mathbb{R}^d$ be the perturbation. Here $d$ is the dimension of the input image, i.e., the number of pixels times the number of color channels. The $\ell_p$ norm of $\delta$ is

<div class="equation" id="equation-1"><span class="katex-display"><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" display="block"><semantics><mtable mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mi>p</mi></msup><mo fence="true">)</mo></mrow><mrow><mn>1</mn><mi mathvariant="normal">/</mi><mi>p</mi></mrow></msup><mo separator="true">,</mo></mrow></mtd><mtd width="50%"></mtd><mtd><mtext>(1)</mtext></mtd></mtr></mtable><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\|\delta\|_p = \left( \sum_{i=1}^{d} |\delta_i|^p \right)^{1/p}, \tag{1}</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="katex-base"><span class="katex-strut" class="mord"><span class="mord">1</span></span><span class="mord">)</span></span></span></span></span></span></div>

for $p \geq 1$. Each choice of $p$ measures size in a different way.

The $\ell_1$ norm adds up the absolute values of all pixel changes. It measures the total change across the image. A small $\ell_1$ norm lets a few pixels change a lot. Most pixels then stay fixed.

The $\ell_2$ norm is the Euclidean distance between the clean image and the perturbed image. It grows with the number of changed pixels and with the size of each change. A small $\ell_2$ norm therefore favors many small changes over a few large ones.

The $\ell_\infty$ norm is the largest single pixel change,

<div class="equation" id="equation-2"><span class="katex-display"><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" display="block"><semantics><mtable mathvariant="normal">∞</mi></msub><mo>=</mo><munder><mrow><mi>max</mi><mo>⁡</mo></mrow><mi>i</mi></munder><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><msub><mi>δ</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><mi mathvariant="normal">∣</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">.</mi></mrow></mtd><mtd width="50%"></mtd><mtd><mtext>(2)</mtext></mtd></mtr></mtable><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\|\delta\|_\infty = \max_i |\delta_i|. \tag{2}</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="katex-base"><span class="katex-strut" style="height:1em;vertical-align:-0.25em;"></span><span class="mord">∥</span><span class="mord mathnormal" style="margin-right:0.0379em;">δ</span><span class="mord"><span class="mord">∥</span><span class="vlist" style="height:0.15em;"><span></span></span></span></span></span></span><span class="mspace" style="margin-right:0.2778em;"></span><span class="mrel">=</span><span class="mspace" class="mop">max</span></span></span></span><span class="vlist-s">​</span></span><span class="vlist-r"><span class="vlist" style="height:0.7277em;"><span></span></span></span></span></span><span class="mspace" style="margin-right:0.1667em;"></span><span class="mord">∣</span><span class="mord"><span class="mord mathnormal" style="margin-right:0.0379em;">δ</span><span class="msupsub"><span class="vlist-t vlist-t2"><span class="vlist-r"><span class="vlist" style="height:0.3117em;"><span style="top:-2.55em;margin-left:-0.0379em;margin-right:0.05em;"><span class="pstrut" style="height:2.7em;"></span><span class="katex-sizing reset-size6 size3 mtight"><span class="mord mathnormal mtight">i</span></span></span></span><span class="vlist-s">​</span></span><span class="vlist-r"><span class="vlist" style="height:0.15em;"><span></span></span></span></span></span></span><span class="mord">∣.</span></span><span class="katex-tag"><span class="katex-strut" style="height:1.4777em;vertical-align:-0.7277em;"></span><span class="mord text"><span class="mord">(</span><span class="mord"><span class="mord">2</span></span><span class="mord">)</span></span></span></span></span></span></div>

It does not count how many pixels change. It only bounds how far any one pixel can move. A small $\ell_\infty$ norm lets every pixel change at once. Each pixel then changes by only a small amount.

Most early attacks and defenses fix a norm and a budget $\epsilon$. The perturbation must satisfy $\|\delta\|_p \leq \epsilon$. The budget $\epsilon$ stays small so the perturbation stays imperceptible to a human. These three norms often define the perturbation constraints of conventional norm-bounded adversarial examples.

<h3 id="section-2-2">2.2 Norm-Bounded vs. Unrestricted Adversarial Examples: Definitions</h3>

The core definitions in this section follow <a class="cite" href="#ref-uae_origin">Song et al. (2018)</a>, which first formally established the concept of unrestricted adversarial examples.

<strong>Image domain and oracle:</strong> Let $I$ denote the set of all realistic images, defined as inputs that appear plausible to human observers. We assume an oracle $o: I \rightarrow \{1,2,...,K\}$ that assigns ground-truth labels consistent with human judgment.

<strong>Classifier:</strong> A classifier $f: I \rightarrow \{1,2,...,K\}$ maps images to discrete class labels. Ideally, for all $x \in I$, the prediction $f(x)$ matches the oracle label $o(x)$.

<strong>Norm-bounded adversarial examples:</strong> Norm-bounded adversarial examples are constructed by adding a perturbation $\delta$, constrained by an $\ell_p$-norm, to a correctly classified image $x \in I$ where $f(x) = o(x)$ <a class="cite" href="#ref-fgsm">Goodfellow et al. (2015)</a>; <a class="cite" href="#ref-madry_at">Madry et al. (2018)</a>; <a class="cite" href="#ref-cw_attack">Carlini &amp; Wagner (2016)</a>; <a class="cite" href="#ref-autoattack">Croce &amp; Hein (2020)</a>. The resulting example $x^*$ fools the classifier while the ground-truth label is preserved: $y = o(x) = o(x^*)$, as in Eq (<a href="#equation-3">3</a>). The norm bound, typically $\ell_\infty$ or $\ell_2$, serves as a proxy for perceptual imperceptibility.

<div class="equation" id="equation-3"><span class="katex-display"><span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" display="block"><semantics><mtable stretchy="false">(</mo><msup><mi>x</mi><mo>∗</mo></msup><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mo mathvariant="normal">≠</mo><mi>y</mi><mspace width="1em"/><mtext>and</mtext><mspace width="1em"/><mo <strong>Unrestricted adversarial examples:</strong> UAEs carry no $\ell_p$-norm constraint. The modification may apply a transformation to a clean image $x \in I$ or synthesize $x^*$ from noise, provided the result looks like a legitimate member of class $y$ to the oracle while the classifier assigns a different label. Formally, $x^* \in I$ is an unrestricted adversarial example if:

text"><span class="mord">(</span><span class="mord"><span class="mord">4</span></span><span class="mord">)</span></span></span></span></span></span></div>

The term <em>unrestricted</em> reflects that there are no constraints on how $x^*$ is produced, beyond the requirement that it remain a plausible image ($x^* \in I$). Note that this definition, taken from <a class="cite" href="#ref-uae_origin">Song et al. (2018)</a>, is satisfied by norm-bounded adversarial examples as well. What distinguishes UAEs in practice is the absence of an $\ell_p$ constraint on how $x^\ast$ is produced. Section <a href="#section-2-4">2.4</a> states the scoping criterion we use throughout to draw that line.

<strong>Visual difference between norm-bounded and unrestricted adversarial examples.</strong> The definitions of these two types of adversarial example above draw a precise mathematical boundary: norm-bounded adversarial examples must satisfy $\lVert\delta\rVert_p\leq\epsilon$ in pixel space, while UAEs require only that the result remain a plausible image according to the oracle. Figure <a href="#figure-1">1</a> makes this boundary concrete on a single source image.

In the top row, a conventional $\ell_p$-bounded perturbation consists of high-frequency pixel noise. The adversarial signal is superimposed on an otherwise clean image and is semantically empty: examined in isolation, $\delta$ carries no recognizable visual content. The perturbation succeeds by exploiting statistical irregularities that are imperceptible to humans but consequential to classifiers. In the bottom row, three of the five UAE categories illustrate the qualitative range of modifications that fall outside the $\ell_p$ constraint. Each category is represented by a small selection of examples; a full account of methods within each category appears in Section <a href="#section-3">3</a>.

In all three displayed cases, a human observer assigns the correct label while the classifier does not. The two remaining UAE categories, distance metric-based and physical parameter-based, are omitted because their perturbations are less visually distinguishable in a static illustration. The figure establishes the qualitative nature of the distinction, not a survey of the taxonomy. That distinction carries a direct implication for defense: because UAE perturbations are woven into semantically meaningful image properties rather than added as a separable noise layer, the signal-removal logic underlying $\ell_p$ calibrated defenses does not apply to them. Section <a href="#section-4">4</a> develops this argument in detail.

<h3 id="section-2-3">2.3 Attack Objectives and Threat Models:</h3>

Although this survey does not organize its taxonomy around conventional threat-model categories as prior surveys have done, these concepts remain foundational to the adversarial examples literature and are introduced here as a prerequisite for the sections that follow. Two dimensions characterize any adversarial attack independently of the properties of the adversarial example itself: the attack objective and the degree of access the attacker holds with respect to the target model.

The <em>attack objective</em> defines the success criterion. An <em>untargeted</em> attack requires only that the classifier misclassify the input, i.e., $f(x^*) \neq y$. A <em>targeted</em> attack additionally specifies a particular incorrect class $t \neq y$ and requires $f(x^*) = t$. Targeted attacks are strictly harder to achieve, a difficulty compounded in the black-box setting. Generative UAE methods that synthesize images from scratch partially circumvent this by conditioning generation on the desired class, making targeted objectives more tractable than they are under direct perturbation.

The <em>attacker’s access</em> to the target model determines the optimization strategies available. In a <em>white-box</em> setting, the attacker has full knowledge of the target model’s architecture, weights, and gradients, enabling direct gradient-based optimization. In a <em>black-box</em> setting, the attacker can only observe model outputs for chosen inputs. Such attacks typically exploit <em>transferability</em>, the empirical finding that adversarial examples crafted against a surrogate model often fool a separate target model <a class="cite" href="#ref-momentum-iterative">Dong et al. (2018)</a>, or use query-based gradient estimation <a class="cite" href="#ref-square-attack">Andriushchenko et al. (2020)</a>. A <em>gray-box</em> setting lies between the two: partial information is available, such as the model architecture without the weights. These distinctions apply equally to UAE attacks and to norm-bounded attacks, and we note the relevant threat model when discussing specific methods in Section <a href="#section-3">3</a>.

<h3 id="section-2-4">2.4 Clarifying Related Concepts and Our Scoping in This Survey:</h3>

The literature on UAE is not terminologically uniform. Several of the methods this survey classifies as UAEs appear under alternative labels, most prominently <em>semantic adversarial examples</em>, a designation that reflects a difference in framing rather than a substantive distinction in the attacks themselves. A separate source of potential confusion arises from the surface resemblance between certain UAE categories and <em>out-of-distribution (OOD) data</em>: both describe inputs that depart from the clean training distribution, yet they differ in origin, intent, and the nature of the departure. This subsection briefly characterizes these related terms and clarifies their relationship to the UAE definition adopted in this survey.

<em>(i) Semantic adversarial examples.</em> Semantic adversarial examples are crafted through semantically meaningful modifications rather than additive pixel noise. Representative operations include shifting hue or saturation <a class="cite" href="#ref-color-first-one-cvprw-2018">Hosseini &amp; Poovendran (2018)</a>; <a class="cite" href="#ref-color-colorfool-cvpr-2020">Shamsabadi et al. (2020b)</a>, applying parametric image transformations <a class="cite" href="#ref-color-functional-attack-nips-2019">Laidlaw &amp; Feizi (2019)</a>, editing facial or object attributes <a class="cite" href="#ref-content-editing-semanticadv">Qiu et al. (2020)</a>, and manipulating localized content while preserving the principal class identity <a class="cite" href="#ref-color-semantic-manipulation-iclr-2020">Bhattad et al. (2020)</a>. The perturbation is expressed in a semantic or generative space rather than as a bounded pixel-level displacement. This label reflects a difference in framing rather than a categorical distinction in generation mechanism; as the scoping criterion in item (iv) below establishes, nearly all methods described as semantic adversarial attacks qualify as UAEs and are treated as such in this survey.

<em>(ii) Out-of-distribution (OOD) data.</em> OOD data refers to inputs that differ from the model’s training distribution, such as images captured under different lighting, weather, or viewpoint conditions, or samples from previously unseen categories <a class="cite" href="#ref-naeweather2">Taori et al. (2020)</a>; <a class="cite" href="#ref-style-augmentation-model-based">Robey et al. (2020)</a>. Critically, OOD data arises from natural distribution shift rather than adversarial intent, and a model’s failure on such inputs reflects a generalization gap rather than an adversarial vulnerability. Robustness to OOD data is therefore studied separately from adversarial robustness and falls outside the scope of this survey.

<em>(iii) Adversarially generated OOD-like inputs.</em> A subtler case arises when an attacker deliberately synthesizes OOD-like inputs, such as adversarial weather, lighting, or environmental conditions, via an optimization procedure designed to cause misclassification while preserving the oracle label. Such inputs are OOD-like in appearance but adversarial in intent. Because their perturbation lies beyond any $\ell_p$-norm bound in pixel space, they are distinguishable from naturally occurring OOD data in both origin and purpose. They are placed within UAE scope under the criterion defined below.

<em>(iv) Our scoping criterion.</em> This survey uses the <strong>nature of the perturbation, rather than the visual magnitude of the change</strong>, as the defining criterion. An adversarial input qualifies as a UAE whenever its modification <em>cannot be captured by an $\ell_p$-norm bound in pixel space</em>: geometric transformations, color or style manipulations, attribute edits, generative synthesis from latent noise, and image-to-image translation all qualify.

These three cases resolve cleanly under the criterion above. Semantic adversarial examples and adversarially generated OOD-like inputs are subsumed within UAE scope; naturally occurring OOD data is not. Throughout the remainder of this survey, the term UAE refers to any adversarial input whose perturbation is not expressible as an $\ell_p$-bounded pixel-space change, regardless of the label under which it appears in the source literature.

<h2 id="section-3">3 Taxonomy of Unrestricted Adversarial Examples</h2>

<figure id="figure-4"><a class="figure-link" href="/blog/beyond-the-lp-ball/figure-4.webp"><img src="/blog/beyond-the-lp-ball/figure-4.webp" alt="Timeline from 2017 to 2026 of representative methods in the five UAE categories, arranged by perturbation flexibility." width="1950" height="1117" loading="lazy" decoding="async"></a><figcaption><strong>Figure 4:</strong> Temporal evolution of UAE generation techniques. Representative methods from each of the five taxonomy categories, plotted by year of appearance (x-axis) against perturbation flexibility (y-axis, Section <a href="#section-5-3">5.3</a>): the freedom each category grants the perturbation in the absence of an <span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><semantics><mrow><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">ℓ</mi><mi>p</mi></msub></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\ell_p</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="katex-base"><span class="katex-strut" style="height:0.9805em;vertical-align:-0.2861em;"></span><span class="mord"><span class="mord">ℓ</span><span class="msupsub"><span class="vlist-t vlist-t2"><span class="vlist-r"><span class="vlist" style="height:0.1514em;"><span style="top:-2.55em;margin-left:0em;margin-right:0.05em;"><span class="pstrut" style="height:2.7em;"></span><span class="katex-sizing reset-size6 size3 mtight"><span class="mord mathnormal mtight">p</span></span></span></span><span class="vlist-s">​</span></span><span class="vlist-r"><span class="vlist" style="height:0.2861em;"><span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span> bound. Vertical position reflects the broad trend of a category, not the exact flexibility of any individual method within it.</figcaption></figure>

<figure id="figure-5"><a class="figure-link" href="/blog/beyond-the-lp-ball/figure-5.webp"><img src="/blog/beyond-the-lp-ball/figure-5.webp" alt="Tree diagram of the UAE taxonomy: five categories, their subcategories, and the representative methods in each." width="2000" height="2503" loading="lazy" decoding="async"></a><figcaption><strong>Figure 5:</strong> Taxonomy of Unrestricted Adversarial Examples. (Methods in italics do not strictly meet the UAE criterion of Section 2.4 but are surveyed for methodological continuity.)</figcaption></figure>

UAEs pose a distinctive challenge in adversarial machine learning: unlike attacks that rely on small, norm-bounded perturbations, they use complex, high-level transformations that preserve semantic fidelity while deceiving DNNs. The diversity of techniques and the absence of a fixed perturbation budget make systematic categorization difficult.

We organize UAEs into five categories based on their generation technique: <strong>transformation-based</strong>, <strong>distance metric-based</strong>, <strong>physical parameter-based</strong>, <strong>style transfer and content editing-based</strong>, and <strong>generative model-based</strong>.

<strong>Transformation-Based UAEs:</strong> This category generates adversarial examples via input-space transformations that directly alter pixel values or spatial structures. Typical transformations include color shifts <a class="cite" href="#ref-color-first-one-cvprw-2018">Hosseini &amp; Poovendran (2018)</a>, photometric properties modification <a class="cite" href="#ref-light-ARA">Zhang et al. (2024)</a>, filtering <a class="cite" href="#ref-filter-color-ace">Zhao et al. (2020b)</a>; <a class="cite" href="#ref-filter-learnable">Shamsabadi et al. (2021)</a>, and geometric modifications <a class="cite" href="#ref-geometric-engstrom">Engstrom et al. (2019)</a>. These perturbations often introduce noticeable changes in the pixel domain, yet the semantic content of the image remains intact: the human-assigned label is unchanged, while the alterations are sufficient to deceive machine learning models, exposing their sensitivity to non-semantic variations.

<strong>Distance Metric-Based UAEs:</strong> Rather than altering the perturbation procedure, this category redefines the constraint itself, replacing the $\ell_p$ ball with metrics that more faithfully reflect human visual perception, such as Wasserstein distance <a class="cite" href="#ref-distance-metric-wassestein">Wong et al. (2019)</a>, CIEDE2000 <a class="cite" href="#ref-distance-metric-percl">Zhao et al. (2020a)</a>, and LPIPS <a class="cite" href="#ref-distance-metric-lpips">Laidlaw et al. (2021)</a>. The reformulation admits larger pixel-space distortions in regions where human vision is insensitive, yielding adversarial examples that appear natural despite substantial deviations under conventional norms. Models trained to resist $\ell_p$-bounded attacks generalize poorly once the metric is redefined, indicating that $\ell_p$ norms do not adequately capture imperceptibility.

<strong>Physical Parameter-Based UAEs:</strong> This category introduces adversarial perturbations to the intrinsic 3D physical properties of objects or scenes rather than modifying 2D image pixels <a class="cite" href="#ref-physical-parameter-based-first-work">Zeng et al. (2019)</a>; <a class="cite" href="#ref-physical-parameter-based-TT3D">Huang et al. (2024)</a>. These approaches support physically realizable attacks and enable adversarial robustness evaluation in real-world settings.

<strong>Style Transfer and Content Editing-Based UAEs:</strong> This category produces UAEs by manipulating the artistic style <a class="cite" href="#ref-style-fusion-AdvST">Wang et al. (2023c)</a>; <a class="cite" href="#ref-style-perturbation-feature-space">Xu et al. (2021)</a>; <a class="cite" href="#ref-style-perturbation-gemetric-aware">Liu et al. (2023a)</a> or high-level semantic attributes of an image <a class="cite" href="#ref-content-editing-parametric-transformation">Joshi et al. (2019)</a>; <a class="cite" href="#ref-content-editing-instruct2attack">Liu et al. (2023b)</a>, often through generative models or feature space transformations, yielding natural-looking changes that fool models.

<strong>Generative Model-Based UAEs:</strong> This category uses generative models, including GANs, VAEs, normalizing flows, and diffusion models, to synthesize adversarial examples. These models generate adversarial examples from scratch <a class="cite" href="#ref-advdiff">Dai et al. (2024)</a>; <a class="cite" href="#ref-venom">Kuurila-Zhang et al. (2025)</a>, perturb existing images within their latent representations <a class="cite" href="#ref-diffattack">Chen et al. (2024</a>; <a class="cite" href="#ref-advdiffuser">2023a)</a>, or produce the adversarial perturbation itself <a class="cite" href="#ref-generating-perturbations-ijcai">Xiao et al. (2018a)</a>, typically yielding highly realistic outputs.

The five categories did not emerge simultaneously, nor have they remained equally active. Figure <a href="#figure-4">4</a> plots representative methods from each category by year against perturbation flexibility, and the resulting pattern reveals a field whose structure is governed less by adversarial machine learning itself than by the generative technology available at each moment. The vertical axis here captures the general tendency of each category; it is not an exact ranking of individual methods, since a single content editing method <a class="cite" href="#ref-content-editing-instruct2attack">Liu et al. (2023b)</a>; <a class="cite" href="#ref-content-editding-ACA">Chen et al. (2023b)</a> may grant more perturbation freedom than a given generative-model-based one <a class="cite" href="#ref-genmodel-reference-advgan++">Jandial et al. (2019)</a>; <a class="cite" href="#ref-uae_origin">Song et al. (2018)</a>. Section <a href="#section-5-3">5.3</a> returns to this question with a finer-grained organization built around the constraints imposed on the perturbation rather than the technique used to generate it. Transformation-based and generative-model-based UAEs both emerged around 2017–2018, but only the generative branch has sustained development to the present. Transformation-based work developed steadily through a peak around 2020 and tapers off after 2024 (Figure <a href="#figure-2">2</a>). The former requires no specialized generative infrastructure and therefore remains viable independent of architectural trends, while the latter has benefited directly from successive advances in generative modeling, each new backbone supplying greater expressive capacity for adversarial synthesis. The remaining three categories are more temporally constrained: distance metric-based, physical parameter-based, and style transfer and content editing-based methods each exhibit concentrated activity within a narrow window before subsiding, having substantially characterized their respective perturbation spaces without the continual technological renewal that sustains the other two. The style transfer and content editing category offers the clearest illustration of this coupling. For example, the style transfer-based UAEs coincide with the period when StyleGAN <a class="cite" href="#ref-styleGAN">Karras et al. (2019)</a> dominated controllable image synthesis. After 2022, diffusion models <a class="cite" href="#ref-stable_diffusion">Rombach et al. (2022)</a> became capable enough that generative attacks in diffusion latent spaces could perform the same targeted style and content manipulations <a class="cite" href="#ref-content-ediging-clip2protect">Shamshad et al. (2023)</a>; <a class="cite" href="#ref-content-editing-instruct2attack">Liu et al. (2023b)</a>; <a class="cite" href="#ref-content-editding-ACA">Chen et al. (2023b)</a>. By 2024, the generative-model-based category had absorbed most of this line of work. It is now the most active of the five categories, and it continues to benefit as the underlying generative models improve. The same pattern holds across categories. Each one becomes active when its enabling technology arrives, and each plateaus when that technology is superseded. UAE research therefore tracks the progress of generative modeling.

Figure <a href="#figure-5">5</a> lays out the full taxonomy as a hierarchy, expanding each of the five categories into the specific mechanisms and representative methods it comprises. Within transformation-based UAEs, for instance, the tree separates color manipulation, photometric modification, filter-based transformation, and geometric and spatial transformation. This hierarchical view maps the rest of the section. Each part treats one category and its subdivisions in turn.

<h3 id="section-3-1">3.1 Transformation-Based UAEs</h3>

Transformation-based UAEs operate directly in the input space by modifying pixel values or spatial structure through conventional image-processing transformations rather than additive, norm-bounded noise. Because these transformations reflect natural variations in real photographs, they can induce substantial $\ell_p$ distortions while preserving human-perceived labels, placing them largely outside the threat models assumed by $\ell_p$-based defenses. Figure <a href="#figure-6">6</a> shows representative examples of transformation-based UAEs. We categorize this class by the visual attribute targeted: <strong>color manipulation</strong>, which alters hue, saturation, and color distributions; <strong>photometric modification</strong>, which perturbs imaging properties such as illumination, vignetting, and exposure; <strong>filter-based transformation</strong>, which incorporates perturbations through conventional image filters; and <strong>geometric and spatial transformation</strong>, which modifies image structure rather than pixel values. Although these approaches exploit different dimensions of human perceptual tolerance, they share a common principle: perturbations grounded in natural image transformations can remain perceptually plausible while achieving greater robustness than pixel-level noise.

<figure id="figure-6"><a class="figure-link" href="/blog/beyond-the-lp-ball/figure-6.webp"><img src="/blog/beyond-the-lp-ball/figure-6.webp" alt="Grid of clean and adversarial image pairs for color manipulation, photometric, filter-based and geometric UAEs." width="2000" height="726" loading="lazy" decoding="async"></a><figcaption><strong>Figure 6:</strong> Qualitative examples of transformation-based UAEs. Each panel shows two example from one subcategory: the top image is the benign input and the bottom image the corresponding adversarial example. The four subcategories are (a) color manipulation, (b) photometric properties modification, (c) filter-based transformation, and (d) geometric and spatial transformation. Images are from the cited works.</figcaption></figure>

<h4 id="section-3-1-1">3.1.1 Color Manipulations</h4>

Color manipulation-based UAEs hold a distinctive position in the adversarial landscape. Unlike $\ell_p$-norm bounded attacks, they introduce large, visible color changes that still preserve semantic content. This property has led prior work to call them <strong>semantic adversarial examples</strong> <a class="cite" href="#ref-color-negative-images">Hosseini et al. (2017)</a>; <a class="cite" href="#ref-color-first-one-cvprw-2018">Hosseini &amp; Poovendran (2018)</a>; <a class="cite" href="#ref-color-semantic-manipulation-iclr-2020">Bhattad et al. (2020)</a>. The central design challenge is to craft color perturbations that both deceive classifiers and remain perceptually plausible to humans. Research in this area has progressed along two key axes. The first is <em>spatial granularity</em>, which ranges from global, uniform perturbations to semantically localized ones. The second is <em>optimization strategy</em>, which ranges from fixed rules and random search to gradient-based learning and data-driven priors.

<strong>Establishing the concept.</strong> The foundational work <a class="cite" href="#ref-color-negative-images">Hosseini et al. (2017)</a> demonstrated this vulnerability with striking simplicity. Inverting pixel intensities (“negative images”) reliably fools CNNs while remaining trivially recognizable to humans. SemanticAdv <a class="cite" href="#ref-color-first-one-cvprw-2018">Hosseini &amp; Poovendran (2018)</a> extended this by applying random shifts to the Hue and Saturation channels in HSV space. This introduced tunable but coarse perturbations. These early methods confirmed that color-based adversarial attacks are viable. However, they often produced perceptually implausible results, such as green skies and purple grass. This motivated more principled approaches.

<strong>Formalizing the threat model.</strong> Two concurrent works from 2019 defined distinct but complementary perspectives on the color manipulation threat model. ReColorAdv <a class="cite" href="#ref-color-functional-attack-nips-2019">Laidlaw &amp; Feizi (2019)</a> formalized color manipulation as a learnable global mapping function in perceptually uniform CIELUV space, regularized for smoothness. This formulation captures large yet perceptually consistent changes. Combined with $\ell_p$-bounded attacks, it yielded the strongest known attacks against adversarially trained models at the time. The blind-spot attack <a class="cite" href="#ref-color-blind-spot-iclr-2019">Zhang et al. (2019b)</a> takes a philosophically different approach. Rather than crafting perceptually plausible colors, it applies simple global transformations such as contrast scaling and background shifting. These transformations push inputs into “blind spots,” regions outside the training distribution but still semantically valid. It then applies a standard C&amp;W attack <a class="cite" href="#ref-cw_attack">Carlini &amp; Wagner (2016)</a> to the displaced image. This exposes a fundamental generalization gap in adversarially trained models. Such models fail to generalize even to perceptually valid inputs that lie slightly off the training manifold.

<strong>Semantic-aware colorization.</strong> The largest advances in this category came from adding semantic segmentation to the attack pipeline. ColorFool <a class="cite" href="#ref-color-colorfool-cvpr-2020">Shamsabadi et al. (2020b)</a> segments the image. It then restricts perturbations to manually defined natural color ranges for each semantic category in Lab color space <a class="cite" href="#ref-lab-color-space">Ruderman et al. (1998)</a>. For example, it allows only plausible sky colors for sky regions. This produces much greater perceptual realism than earlier methods. cAdv <a class="cite" href="#ref-color-semantic-manipulation-iclr-2020">Bhattad et al. (2020)</a> instead approaches semantic awareness through a generative model. It de-colorizes the input to grayscale. It then re-colorizes it with a neural colorization model fine-tuned per image. The adversarial signal is embedded within the colorization process. This also improves robustness against defenses such as JPEG compression <a class="cite" href="#ref-defense-jpeg-compression">Das et al. (2017)</a> and feature squeezing <a class="cite" href="#ref-defense-feature-squeezing">Xu et al. (2018)</a>. Natural Color Fool (NCF) <a class="cite" href="#ref-color-natural-colorfool-nips-2022">Yuan et al. (2022)</a> goes further. It replaces manually defined color bounds with data-driven color distribution libraries for each semantic class. These are applied through a learnable transfer matrix optimized by adversarial loss. NCF achieves strong black-box transferability while maintaining perceptual quality.

<strong>Summary:</strong> Color manipulation-based UAEs have evolved from coarse global transformations toward semantically grounded, data-driven attacks. A recurring tension is the trade-off between perturbation freedom and perceptual plausibility. Methods gained greater flexibility through learnable functions, generative models, and data-driven priors. Constraining that flexibility in semantically meaningful ways then became the central challenge. Two complementary insights run through this line of work. The first is semantic color coherence, exploited by ColorFool and NCF. The second is distributional generalization failure, revealed by the blind-spot attack. Together they broaden the scope of what constitutes a valid color-based adversarial attack.

<h4 id="section-3-1-2">3.1.2 Photometric Properties Modification</h4>

Color manipulation alters hue, saturation, or color distribution. Photometric modification attacks instead target intrinsic imaging attributes such as illumination, vignetting, lightness, and exposure. These attributes arise naturally from camera optics and scene lighting. Photometric variation is ubiquitous in real-world photography. Perturbations grounded in such phenomena are therefore inherently difficult to distinguish from natural image variation. Four representative methods, AVA <a class="cite" href="#ref-light-AVA">TIAN et al. (2021)</a>, ARA <a class="cite" href="#ref-light-ARA">Zhang et al. (2024)</a>, ALA <a class="cite" href="#ref-light-ALA">Huang et al. (2023)</a>, and Jadena <a class="cite" href="#ref-light-jedena">Gao et al. (2022)</a>, appeared within a narrow time window. They are parallel developments rather than a strict evolutionary sequence.

<strong>Physical optics-grounded attacks.</strong> AVA <a class="cite" href="#ref-light-AVA">TIAN et al. (2021)</a> and ARA <a class="cite" href="#ref-light-ARA">Zhang et al. (2024)</a> both anchor their attacks in physical models of how light interacts with imaging systems. AVA embeds adversarial perturbations within vignetting, a radial darkening effect common in real photography. It does this by optimizing physical parameters such as focal length and illumination factors. This exploits a naturally occurring optical artifact as camouflage. ARA targets face recognition by manipulating facial illumination. It introduces three variants of increasing physical commitment. AQ-ARA optimizes lighting parameters digitally through adversarial feedback. AP-ARA learns adversarial lighting patterns with a DNN. Phy-ARA physically instantiates the attack using a real relighting device. These works show that physical lighting phenomena constitute a largely overlooked adversarial surface. This surface is particularly relevant for real-world deployment.

<strong>Perceptual insensitivity as an attack surface.</strong> ALA <a class="cite" href="#ref-light-ALA">Huang et al. (2023)</a> and Jadena <a class="cite" href="#ref-light-jedena">Gao et al. (2022)</a> exploit dimensions along which human perception is relatively insensitive. ALA modifies image lightness through a differentiable piecewise parametric filter. It preserves shape and color cues. Lightness shifts are pervasive in real photography yet minimally affect human recognition. Jadena takes a different direction. It targets co-salient object detection (CoSOD) rather than classification. It jointly applies exposure manipulation and pixel-wise noise to suppress saliency cues across an image group. Jadena is the first adversarial attack on CoSOD. It shows that photometric perturbations can be adapted to disrupt complex, group-level visual reasoning tasks beyond simple recognition.

<strong>Summary:</strong> These methods collectively show that the adversarial perturbation space extends well beyond pixel-wise noise and color shifts. Photometric attributes grounded in real imaging phenomena offer a rich and under-defended attack surface. These attributes include lens vignetting, illumination changes, lightness shifts, and exposure levels. They form such a surface precisely because they exploit naturally occurring image variations that are indistinguishable from benign photographic conditions. Color manipulation approaches are less tightly coupled to physical imaging processes. Photometric attacks couple more tightly to these processes. This improves both physical realizability and stealth.

<h4 id="section-3-1-3">3.1.3 Filter-based Transformations</h4>

EdgeFool <a class="cite" href="#ref-filter-edgefool">Shamsabadi et al. (2020a)</a>, ACE <a class="cite" href="#ref-filter-color-ace">Zhao et al. (2020b)</a>, and FilterFool <a class="cite" href="#ref-filter-learnable">Shamsabadi et al. (2021)</a> share a common design principle. Adversarial perturbations are embedded within conventional image processing operations rather than added as explicit noise. This makes the result resemble ordinary image enhancement. The three methods form a coherent progression. They move from a specialized single-purpose filter to a general learnable framework.

EdgeFool <a class="cite" href="#ref-filter-edgefool">Shamsabadi et al. (2020a)</a> introduces the paradigm. It decomposes images into structural and residual components. It then applies adversarial perturbations to the detail (residual) component in the L channel of Lab space <a class="cite" href="#ref-lab-color-space">Ruderman et al. (1998)</a>. This effectively disguises them as edge sharpening. ACE <a class="cite" href="#ref-filter-color-ace">Zhao et al. (2020b)</a> shifts to gradient-based optimization of a differentiable color filter. It replaces the random search of earlier color attacks <a class="cite" href="#ref-color-first-one-cvprw-2018">Hosseini &amp; Poovendran (2018)</a>; <a class="cite" href="#ref-color-colorfool-cvpr-2020">Shamsabadi et al. (2020b)</a> with efficient gradient descent. It also integrates color enhancement regularization to ensure artifact-free outputs. FilterFool <a class="cite" href="#ref-filter-learnable">Shamsabadi et al. (2021)</a> completes the progression. It generalizes the framework to support arbitrary traditional filters such as detail enhancement, gamma correction, and logarithmic transformation within a unified learnable architecture. Its key addition is a semantic adversarial loss that targets semantically distant misclassification classes. These classes are defined through WordNet hierarchies on ImageNet. This makes attacks more disruptive than label-agnostic methods.

<strong>Summary:</strong> This line of work establishes filter operations for adversarial perturbations. It progresses from a single-purpose edge filter (EdgeFool) to gradient-efficient color enhancement (ACE) to a general-purpose, semantically-aware framework (FilterFool). The common payoff is adversarial examples that are visually indistinguishable from legitimately processed images. This improves imperceptibility and transferability beyond what norm-bounded attacks achieve.

<h4 id="section-3-1-4">3.1.4 Geometric and Spatial Transformations</h4>

Geometric and spatial transformation attacks manipulate image structure rather than pixel values. They produce perturbations that can be large in $\ell_p$ distance yet remain perceptually natural. They fall largely outside the threat models assumed by $\ell_p$-based defenses. Models trained to handle pixel noise are therefore poorly equipped to handle them. The six methods in this category form a clear three-stage progression.

<strong>Establishing spatial vulnerability.</strong> Engstrom et al. <a class="cite" href="#ref-geometric-engstrom">Engstrom et al. (2019)</a> and ManiFool <a class="cite" href="#ref-geometric-manifool">Kanbak et al. (2018)</a> established the core finding. Simple rigid transformations such as rotation, translation, and scaling reliably fool DNNs. These transformations remain perceptually natural. A key insight from Engstrom et al. <a class="cite" href="#ref-geometric-engstrom">Engstrom et al. (2019)</a> is that the loss landscape under spatial perturbations is highly non-concave. This makes first-order optimization unreliable. It therefore motivates random search with majority-vote aggregation. ManiFool <a class="cite" href="#ref-geometric-manifool">Kanbak et al. (2018)</a> formalizes this as an invariance score, the minimal transformation needed to alter a prediction. It also shows that adversarial training on worst-case geometric transformations improves model robustness. Together, these works raised the question of how to go beyond rigid, global transformations.

<strong>Dense flow-field deformations.</strong> stAdv <a class="cite" href="#ref-geometric-stadv">Xiao et al. (2018b)</a> answered this by learning a per-pixel flow field. This enables smooth, localized spatial warping through differentiable bilinear interpolation. Its critical finding is that these spatial attacks remain effective against PGD adversarial training <a class="cite" href="#ref-madry_at">Madry et al. (2018)</a> and other $\ell_p$-specific defenses. This confirms that spatial robustness requires dedicated treatment. ADef <a class="cite" href="#ref-geometric-adef">Alaifari et al. (2019)</a> arrives at an approach similar to stAdv, iteratively constructing deformations and optionally applying Gaussian smoothing to the flow field. This concentrates deformations around object boundaries. Chroma-Shift <a class="cite" href="#ref-geometric-chromashift">Aydin et al. (2021)</a> explored a further dimension. It restricts the flow field to the chrominance channels (U, V) in YUV space while preserving luminance. Chrominance-only spatial deformations carry sufficient adversarial energy to fool classifiers. This result challenges the assumption that luminance dominates adversarial vulnerability. Human perceptual studies confirm that these deformations are imperceptible.

<strong>Extending to 3D.</strong> ViewFool <a class="cite" href="#ref-geometric-viewfool">Dong et al. (2022)</a> extends the paradigm into 3D. It uses Neural Radiance Fields (NeRF) <a class="cite" href="#ref-nerf">Mildenhall et al. (2021)</a> to generate adversarially optimized camera viewpoints. Rather than manipulating a 2D image, it learns a distribution of adversarial poses through an entropy-regularized objective. This produces physically realizable and continuously parameterized attacks. Evaluation across 40 classifiers on the ImageNet-V <a class="cite" href="#ref-geometric-viewfool">Dong et al. (2022)</a> benchmark reveals widespread sensitivity to viewpoint shifts. Transformer-based models <a class="cite" href="#ref-vit">Dosovitskiy et al. (2021)</a> are on average more robust to viewpoint change than CNNs <a class="cite" href="#ref-he2016deep">He et al. (2016)</a>. This points to architectural pathways for improving spatial invariance.

<strong>Summary:</strong> Spatial transformation-based UAEs trace a clear trajectory. It begins with the finding that rigid geometric changes fool classifiers (Engstrom, ManiFool). It then moves to dense flow-field deformations that bypass $\ell_p$-specific defenses (stAdv, ADef, Chroma-Shift). It ends with physically grounded 3D viewpoint attacks (ViewFool). Spatial perturbations are fundamentally misaligned with $\ell_p$-norm-based evaluation and defense. This mismatch becomes more consequential as attacks move toward physical deployability.

<h4 id="section-3-1-5">3.1.5 Overview of Transformation-Based UAEs</h4>

A consistent design philosophy emerges across all four subcategories (color manipulation, photometric modification, filter-based enhancement, and geometric deformation). Perturbations grounded in natural image transformations are harder to detect and more robust than norm-bounded pixel noise. Each subcategory exploits a different axis of perceptual tolerance. Yet all produce examples that humans can easily recognize, but classifiers fail. Defenses designed for $\ell_p$-norm-bounded attacks have limited protection against these attacks. The perturbations lie largely outside the threat models assumed by most adversarial training schemes.

<h3 id="section-3-2">3.2 Distance Metric-Based UAEs</h3>

<figure id="figure-7"><a class="figure-link" href="/blog/beyond-the-lp-ball/figure-7.webp"><img src="/blog/beyond-the-lp-ball/figure-7.webp" alt="Five clean and adversarial image pairs for distance metric-based UAEs, each labeled with the method and its distance metric." width="2000" height="908" loading="lazy" decoding="async"></a><figcaption><strong>Figure 7:</strong> Examples of distance metric-based UAEs. Each column shows one clean/adversarial pair produced by a representative method: the clean input in the top row and the resulting adversarial example directly below, with the distance metric that method adopts given in parentheses under the column. From left to right: a Wasserstein ball <a class="cite" href="#ref-distance-metric-wassestein">Wong et al. (2019)</a>, the CIEDE2000 color-difference metric <a class="cite" href="#ref-distance-metric-percl">Zhao et al. (2020a)</a>, the learned LPIPS metric under constrained optimization <a class="cite" href="#ref-distance-metric-lpips">Laidlaw et al. (2021)</a> and under direct perceptual-loss minimization <a class="cite" href="#ref-distance-metric-demiguise">Wang et al. (2021)</a>, and <span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><semantics><mrow><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">ℓ</mi><mi>p</mi></msub></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\ell_p</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="katex-base"><span class="katex-strut" style="height:0.9805em;vertical-align:-0.2861em;"></span><span class="mord"><span class="mord">ℓ</span><span class="msupsub"><span class="vlist-t vlist-t2"><span class="vlist-r"><span class="vlist" style="height:0.1514em;"><span style="top:-2.55em;margin-left:0em;margin-right:0.05em;"><span class="pstrut" style="height:2.7em;"></span><span class="katex-sizing reset-size6 size3 mtight"><span class="mord mathnormal mtight">p</span></span></span></span><span class="vlist-s">​</span></span><span class="vlist-r"><span class="vlist" style="height:0.2861em;"><span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span> optimization augmented with perceptual regularizers <a class="cite" href="#ref-distance-metric-shaddow-attack">Ghiasi et al. (2020)</a>. Images are from the cited works.</figcaption></figure>

The $\ell_p$-norm bound is a poor proxy for visual perception. A spatially uniform shift across all pixels can be large in $\ell_2$ norm while remaining invisible. A small $\ell_\infty$ norm perturbation can be visually noticeable if it introduces sharp, localized artifacts. Distance metric-based UAEs redefine the constraint space itself. They replace $\ell_p$ balls with metrics that reflect what humans actually see. Two directions have emerged. <strong>Wasserstein distance</strong> treats perturbations as mass transport and is inherently spatially aware. <strong>Perceptual distance</strong> measures similarity through established color science or learned neural features. Figure <a href="#figure-7">7</a> shows one clean/adversarial pair per representative method, with the governing distance metric named under each column.

<h4 id="section-3-2-1">3.2.1 Wasserstein Distance</h4>

The Wasserstein Attack <a class="cite" href="#ref-distance-metric-wassestein">Wong et al. (2019)</a> replaces the $\ell_p$ ball with a Wasserstein ball (W-ball). The W-ball is defined as the optimal transport cost of moving pixel mass across spatial locations. The $\ell_p$ norms treat each pixel independently. Instead, Wasserstein distance is spatially aware. It accounts for where pixel changes occur relative to local structure, not merely their magnitude. The attack follows PGD’s iterative procedure. It replaces $\ell_p$ projection with W-ball projection through a modified Sinkhorn iteration <a class="cite" href="#ref-sinkhorn-distance">Cuturi (2013)</a>. The result is structurally coherent, content-aware perturbations. These contrast sharply with the high-frequency noise of $\ell_p$ attacks. They provide a principled basis for smooth, natural-looking adversarial examples.

<h4 id="section-3-2-2">3.2.2 Perceptual Distance</h4>

Perceptual distance-based attacks replace or supplement $\ell_p$ constraints with metrics that quantify visual similarity as humans perceive it. PerC-C&amp;W replaces the $\ell_p$ distance metric with established color science. NPTM <a class="cite" href="#ref-distance-metric-lpips">Laidlaw et al. (2021)</a> and Demiguise Attack <a class="cite" href="#ref-distance-metric-demiguise">Wang et al. (2021)</a> use learned neural features <a class="cite" href="#ref-lpips">Zhang et al. (2018)</a> as the distance metric for perturbations. Shadow Attack <a class="cite" href="#ref-distance-metric-shaddow-attack">Ghiasi et al. (2020)</a> augments the constraint through perceptual regularization without replacing the metric.

<strong>Established color science.</strong> PerC-C&amp;W and PerC-AL <a class="cite" href="#ref-distance-metric-percl">Zhao et al. (2020a)</a> are the first to substitute CIEDE2000 for the $\ell_p$ norm. CIEDE2000 is a human-calibrated color difference formula. PerC-C&amp;W adapts the C&amp;W attack <a class="cite" href="#ref-cw_attack">Carlini &amp; Wagner (2016)</a> to optimize under CIEDE2000 distance. It exploits the metric’s tolerance for larger RGB changes in perceptually uniform regions such as smooth or saturated areas, where human vision is less sensitive. PerC-AL improves efficiency by alternating between the classification loss and the perceptual color loss at each iteration. This accelerates convergence at no cost to effectiveness. Established color science thus offers a principled, human-validated basis for redefining the adversarial constraint.

<strong>Learned neural metrics.</strong> NPTM <a class="cite" href="#ref-distance-metric-lpips">Laidlaw et al. (2021)</a> and the Demiguise Attack <a class="cite" href="#ref-distance-metric-demiguise">Wang et al. (2021)</a> both adopt LPIPS <a class="cite" href="#ref-lpips">Zhang et al. (2018)</a> as their constraint. LPIPS is a learned perceptual similarity metric based on normalized activations from pretrained networks. NPTM introduces two attack variants. The first is PPGD, an LPIPS-constrained PGD. The second is LPA, a Lagrangian relaxation form. NPTM pairs these with a corresponding defense, Perceptual Adversarial Training (PAT). A large-scale human evaluation confirms that LPIPS aligns with human judgment. This validates it as a threat model basis. It also exposes that $\ell_p$-trained defenses generalize poorly to these attacks. The Demiguise Attack takes a complementary angle. Rather than constrained optimization, it directly minimizes a composite of adversarial and LPIPS perceptual loss. This steers perturbations toward high-level semantic features. Perceptual-distance optimization naturally produces structured, feature-rich modifications. These remain visually imperceptible despite large pixel-space distortion.

<strong>Perceptual regularization without metric replacement.</strong> Shadow Attack <a class="cite" href="#ref-distance-metric-shaddow-attack">Ghiasi et al. (2020)</a> takes a different approach. Rather than replacing $\ell_p$ norms with a perceptual metric, it augments standard PGD <a class="cite" href="#ref-madry_at">Madry et al. (2018)</a> with three penalty terms. These penalize total variation for spatial smoothness, mean color for color balance, and inter-channel dissimilarity for structural consistency. These penalties guide optimization toward large-$\ell_p$ perturbations that remain visually natural. The primary target is certified defenses <a class="cite" href="#ref-certified-defense-crown">Zhang et al. (2020)</a>; <a class="cite" href="#ref-rs">Cohen et al. (2019)</a>. These provide provable robustness within $\ell_p$ balls. They are less robust to perturbations that exceed those bounds. Shadow Attack’s success against these defenses exposes a critical gap between formal robustness guarantees and practical visual imperceptibility.

<strong>Summary:</strong> Across this category, $\ell_p$ norms prove an inadequate proxy for perceptual imperceptibility. The alternatives span a wide spectrum. They include precisely calibrated color science (CIEDE2000), data-driven neural features (LPIPS), and informal perceptual regularization (Shadow Attack). A consistent finding is that models and defenses trained under $\ell_p$-norm-bounded threat models generalize poorly to perceptually grounded attacks. This reinforces the need to rethink what constitutes a meaningful adversarial threat model.

<h4 id="section-3-2-3">3.2.3 Overview of Distance Metric-Based UAEs</h4>

A common design principle unites both subcategories (Wasserstein distance and perceptual distance). These methods follow the same process as the conventional $\ell_p$-norm bounded methods. They also often apply the same algorithm, such as PGD and C&amp;W. Only the norm ball it projects onto changes, whether to optimal transport cost (Wasserstein distance), calibrated color difference, or learned feature similarity (perceptual distance). Each alternative metric admits large pixel-space distortions precisely where human vision tolerates them. This yields adversarial examples that stay natural despite large deviations under conventional norms in pixel space.

<h3 id="section-3-3">3.3 Physical Parameter-Based UAEs</h3>

Rather than modifying image pixels, attacks in this category manipulate the underlying physical parameters of a 3D rendering pipeline. These parameters include camera pose, lighting, material properties, and surface geometry. A single parameter change can produce large, non-local pixel shifts that no $\ell_p$ ball can meaningfully bound. This makes physical parameters a natural adversarial constraint space. The category conceptually overlaps with transformation-based attacks. Camera-pose changes are geometric transformations. Illumination changes are photometric adjustments. The category distinguishes itself by treating rendering parameters directly as the perturbation space. Our discussion focuses on the foundational digital-space works that established this paradigm. Subsequent physical-deployment efforts such as TT3D <a class="cite" href="#ref-physical-parameter-based-TT3D">Huang et al. (2024)</a> and Active <a class="cite" href="#ref-physical-parameter-based-active">Suryanto et al. (2023)</a> fall outside this digital-centric survey.

<h4 id="section-3-3-1">3.3.1 Perturb the Physical Parameters of a 3D Renderer</h4>

Two early works define this paradigm. Both perturb physical scene parameters within a 3D rendering pipeline. They differ critically in where they impose the imperceptibility constraint. This distinction determines whether the resulting attack qualifies as a true UAE.

<strong>A pixel-space-constrained start.</strong> Zeng et al. <a class="cite" href="#ref-physical-parameter-based-first-work">Zeng et al. (2019)</a> were among the first to introduce adversarial attack to operate over 3D physical parameters. A 3D scene is rendered into a 2D image. The image is fed to a classifier. End-to-end gradients then flow back to physical attributes. These attributes are surface normals, illumination, and materials in differentiable renderers. They are object color, translation, rotation, and lighting in non-differentiable ones. The method still bounds perturbations using an $\ell_2$ norm in the rendered 2D image. <strong>It therefore introduces the conceptual machinery for parameter-space attacks but does not yet qualify as a true UAE.</strong> Imperceptibility remains measured in pixel space. The authors themselves note the underlying mismatch. A single parameter change can affect many pixels simultaneously through non-local rendering effects. This makes pixel-space norms both awkward and overly restrictive. Despite this, the work established 3D physical parameters as an adversarial attack surface. It prompted follow-ups <a class="cite" href="#ref-physical-parameter-based-TT3D">Huang et al. (2024)</a>; <a class="cite" href="#ref-physical-parameter-based-active">Suryanto et al. (2023)</a> that commit more fully to physically meaningful constraint spaces.

<strong>Completing the conceptual leap.</strong> Liu et al. <a class="cite" href="#ref-physical-parameter-based-parametric-adversaries">Liu et al. (2019)</a> address this mismatch directly by moving the constraint into the parameter space itself. They introduce <em>parametric norm-balls</em>. These are $\ell_\infty$ bounds defined over physical parameters such as environment lighting rather than over rendered pixels. This ties imperceptibility to plausible real-world variation regardless of pixel-space magnitude. The method therefore qualifies as a true UAE. The work also contributes a custom, analytically differentiable renderer. This renderer explicitly models light propagation. It computes exact gradients of pixel colors with respect to physical parameters. This yields substantially better efficiency than <a class="cite" href="#ref-physical-parameter-based-first-work">Zeng et al. (2019)</a>. Adversarial training with these parametric examples improves robustness to real-world physical variation.

<strong>Summary:</strong> The progression from <a class="cite" href="#ref-physical-parameter-based-first-work">Zeng et al. (2019)</a> to <a class="cite" href="#ref-physical-parameter-based-parametric-adversaries">Liu et al. (2019)</a> shows a central principle. When adversarial variation lies in physical parameter space, pixel-space norms are not just imprecise but conceptually misaligned. Parametric norm-balls resolve this by anchoring imperceptibility in physical reality. They produce attacks that are more efficient, more physically meaningful, and properly classifiable as UAEs.

<h3 id="section-3-4">3.4 Style Transfer and Content Editing-Based UAEs</h3>

<figure id="figure-8"><a class="figure-link" href="/blog/beyond-the-lp-ball/figure-8.webp"><img src="/blog/beyond-the-lp-ball/figure-8.webp" alt="Grid of clean and adversarial image pairs for style fusion, style perturbation, style augmentation and content editing UAEs." width="2000" height="730" loading="lazy" decoding="async"></a><figcaption><strong>Figure 8:</strong> Examples of style transfer and content editing-based UAEs. The top row shows clean images and the bottom row the corresponding adversarial images, with the generating method named underneath. The four subcategories are (a) style fusion, which blends the style of an external donor image into the victim image, (b) style perturbation, which drops the donor and instead perturbs channel-wise feature statistics inside the network, (c) style augmentation, which applies the same machinery to synthesize natural style variations for robust training or improved transferability, and (d) content editing, which modifies high-level semantic content rather than low-level style. Images are from the cited works.</figcaption></figure>

Style transfer and content editing-based UAEs change the visual style or semantic content of an image directly. The resulting perturbations are large in magnitude. However, they stay perceptually plausible. Most methods rely on <strong>generative models</strong> such as neural style transfer networks, GANs, VAEs, or diffusion models. This raises the question of <strong>why they are not folded into the next subsection</strong> on <em>Generative Model-Based UAEs</em>. The distinction is not the use of generative machinery itself. Instead, it is the <strong>explicit targeting of <em>style</em> or <em>content</em></strong> as the perturbation axis. Generative model-based attacks treat the generator as a flexible source of adversarial samples. The methods here use it to manipulate well-defined image properties in a targeted way. Style refers to low-level visual attributes such as color, texture, and illumination. Content refers to high-level semantic information such as object identity, facial attributes, and scene composition.

The methods fall into four subcategories, with representative examples shown in Figure <a href="#figure-8">8</a>. Style Fusion blends style from an external reference image into the victim image. Style Perturbation drops the reference image. It manipulates style-related features inside the network directly.
