Style Augmentation extends style transformations into adversarial training and transferability-enhancing techniques. It covers both defenses and attacks. Defenses train on style-varied data. Attacks exploit style to enhance transferability. Content Editing changes high-level semantic attributes through generative models. These models are conditioned on attributes, latent codes, or natural language.

<h4 id="section-3-4-1">3.4.1 Style Fusion</h4>

Style fusion-based attacks transfer the visual style of an external reference image onto a victim image. The resulting adversarial examples carry large but perceptually coherent transformations. The reference image acts as a style donor. Its style is extracted through deep features, typically Gram matrices of intermediate activations. This style is then blended with the victim’s content under a composite loss. Three methods, tAdv <a class="cite" href="#ref-color-semantic-manipulation-iclr-2020">Bhattad et al. (2020)</a>, AdvCam <a class="cite" href="#ref-style-fusion-adversarial-camouflage">Duan et al. (2020)</a>, and AdvST <a class="cite" href="#ref-style-fusion-AdvST">Wang et al. (2023c)</a>, form a clear progression in compositional sophistication. The fourth, Activation Attack <a class="cite" href="#ref-feature-space-attack-Activation-attack">Inkawhich et al. (2019)</a>, applies the same fusion principle in feature space rather than at the image level.

<strong>Progressively richer style fusion.</strong> tAdv <a class="cite" href="#ref-color-semantic-manipulation-iclr-2020">Bhattad et al. (2020)</a> was introduced alongside cAdv in the same paper (Section <a href="#section-3-1-1">3.1.1</a>). It is the simplest formulation. It transfers texture from a reference image of the nearest target class through cross-layer Gram-matrix constraints. It combines a texture-transfer loss with an adversarial classification loss. AdvCam <a class="cite" href="#ref-style-fusion-adversarial-camouflage">Duan et al. (2020)</a> extends this with a four-term objective: style, content, adversarial, and smoothness losses. Here, the style loss replaces the $\ell_p$ norm as the stealthiness constraint. The richer formulation supports flexible attack regions. It also crosses into the physical domain through camouflage styles such as stain patterns applied to stop signs. AdvST <a class="cite" href="#ref-style-fusion-AdvST">Wang et al. (2023c)</a> further refines the paradigm with two task-specific variants. <em>P-Attack</em> uses semantic segmentation maps to guide photorealistic style transfer from natural reference photos that share content with the victim. <em>A-Attack</em> uses Laplace edge extraction to preserve structure when transferring artistic styles whose content does not match.

<strong>Feature-space variant.</strong> Activation Attack (AA) <a class="cite" href="#ref-feature-space-attack-Activation-attack">Inkawhich et al. (2019)</a> applies the fusion principle at the feature level. It minimizes the Euclidean distance between the intermediate-layer activations of the victim image and a randomly sampled source image from the target class. This is done within a white-box surrogate model. The victim’s representation gradually shifts toward the source. AA is primarily a black-box transferability attack rather than a style transfer method. However, its activation-fusion mechanism aligns conceptually with style fusion. It also points toward the style perturbation methods that follow, which drop the reference image entirely.

<strong>Summary:</strong> Style fusion methods evolve along a clear path. tAdv performs texture-only transfer. AdvCam adds multi-loss objectives that explicitly preserve content. AdvST introduces segmentation- and structure-aware variants that support both photorealistic and artistic transfer. Activation Attack carries the fusion idea into feature space. This bridges conceptually to the style perturbation paradigm.

<h4 id="section-3-4-2">3.4.2 Style Perturbation</h4>

Style fusion requires an external reference image. Style perturbation-based attacks do not. They directly manipulate style-related features extracted by the network itself. The working assumption is that classifiers rely too heavily on secondary stylistic cues. These cues are captured by feature statistics such as channel means and standard deviations. Perturbing these statistics in feature space yields adversarial examples that remain natural in pixel space. Two methods illustrate the paradigm. FSA <a class="cite" href="#ref-style-perturbation-feature-space">Xu et al. (2021)</a> establishes the foundational mechanism. The Geometry-Aware Attack (GA) <a class="cite" href="#ref-style-perturbation-gemetric-aware">Liu et al. (2023a)</a> provides a practical enhancement built on top of it.

<strong>Foundational feature-space style attack.</strong> FSA <a class="cite" href="#ref-style-perturbation-feature-space">Xu et al. (2021)</a> operates in two phases. First, a decoder is trained on intra-class image pairs. It learns to translate feature-space style differences back into natural pixel-level changes. These differences are encoded as the mean ($\mu$) and standard deviation ($\sigma$) of intermediate activations. Once trained, the encoder and decoder are frozen. The attack then proceeds by perturbing $\mu$ and $\sigma$. It does so in one of two ways. The <em>feature augmentation attack</em> perturbs them independently within an $\ell_\infty$ ball. The <em>feature interpolation attack</em> moves them toward points on a simplex of style prototypes. Conventional pixel-space defenses are largely ineffective against these feature-space style perturbations. This exposes an attack surface that pixel-based adversarial training does not cover.

<strong>Adaptive budget selection.</strong> The Geometry-Aware Attack (GA) <a class="cite" href="#ref-style-perturbation-gemetric-aware">Liu et al. (2023a)</a> addresses a different limitation. UAE methods produce perceptually natural outputs but often suffer from low transferability. This reflects an intrinsic trade-off between visual quality and cross-model effectiveness. GA tackles this by adaptively selecting a per-image perturbation budget. White-box surrogate models are split into training and validation sets. Perturbations are crafted using the training models. The validation models then guide early stopping at the smallest budget that remains effective. Optimal perturbation magnitudes depend on the input’s geometric position relative to the decision boundary. This position varies per image. GA combines with FSA, input diversity <a class="cite" href="#ref-input-diversity">Xie et al. (2019)</a>, and momentum-iterative methods <a class="cite" href="#ref-momentum-iterative">Dong et al. (2018)</a>. The resulting GA-DMI-FSA pipeline shows that adaptive budgeting can rebalance the transferability/realism trade-off in style-based attacks.

<strong>Summary:</strong> Style perturbation methods drop the reference image. They exploit the model’s own internal style representations. FSA establishes that pixel-space defenses do not transfer to feature-space style attacks. This exposes an under-defended attack surface. GA shows that adapting the perturbation budget per input substantially improves transferability without sacrificing realism.

<h4 id="section-3-4-3">3.4.3 Style Augmentation</h4>

Style augmentation extends style transformations beyond direct adversarial generation. It uses style as an axis for augmenting training or input data. This serves two purposes. In defense, it improves model robustness to natural and adversarial style variations. In attack, it improves the transferability of pixel-bounded perturbations. The methods here are technically a mix. The defense methods generate UAE-like training samples through generative (CVAE <a class="cite" href="#ref-style-augmentation-learning-perturbation-set">Wong &amp; Kolter (2021)</a>) or disentangled style models(Robey et al. <a class="cite" href="#ref-style-augmentation-model-based">Robey et al. (2020)</a>, AdvMix <a class="cite" href="#ref-style-augmentation-AdvMix">Gowal et al. (2020)</a>). Several attack and adversarial-training methods (StyLess <a class="cite" href="#ref-style-augmentation-styless">Liang &amp; Xiao (2023)</a>, STM <a class="cite" href="#ref-style-augmentation-STM">Ge et al. (2023)</a>, SAT <a class="cite" href="#ref-style-augmentation-SAT">Naseer et al. (2022)</a>) operate within $\ell_p$-bounded threat models. These do not strictly produce UAEs. However, their use of style transformations connects directly to the UAE framework. This justifies their inclusion.

<strong>Defense via natural-variation augmentation.</strong> Robey et al. <a class="cite" href="#ref-style-augmentation-model-based">Robey et al. (2020)</a>, CVAE <a class="cite" href="#ref-style-augmentation-learning-perturbation-set">Wong &amp; Kolter (2021)</a>, and AdvMix <a class="cite" href="#ref-style-augmentation-AdvMix">Gowal et al. (2020)</a> share a common goal. They train networks to resist natural, real-world variations such as lighting, weather, motion blur, texture shifts, and deformations. These variations lie inherently outside any $\ell_p$ ball. The three methods differ in how they model the variation. Robey et al. <a class="cite" href="#ref-style-augmentation-model-based">Robey et al. (2020)</a> uses an encoder-decoder that disentangles semantic content from a learned “nuisance space” encoding stylistic and environmental attributes. It is trained on unpaired data. It generates diverse natural variations by manipulating latent factors. CVAE uses paired clean–perturbed data to learn fine-grained perturbations within an $\ell_2$-constrained latent space derived from the prior. Running PGD within this latent space turns the framework into a true UAE generator. It can produce blur, lighting changes, weather effects, and digital artifacts. AdvMix uses StyleGAN <a class="cite" href="#ref-styleGAN">Karras et al. (2019)</a> to disentangle content and style components. It then perturbs each independently and recombines them across source images. This produces semantically meaningful variations without requiring external paired or unpaired data. AdvMix is the first work to explicitly study the interplay between adversarial training and disentangled data augmentation. Together, these methods extend adversarial training beyond norm-bounded perturbations to realistic distributional shifts. They form a conceptual bridge between adversarial robustness and out-of-distribution generalization.

<strong>Style-aware adversarial training.</strong> Stylized Adversarial Training (SAT) <a class="cite" href="#ref-style-augmentation-SAT">Naseer et al. (2022)</a> pairs style-fusion-based augmentation with a max-margin contrastive loss. This augmentation is analogous to AdvCam <a class="cite" href="#ref-style-fusion-adversarial-camouflage">Duan et al. (2020)</a> (Section <a href="#section-3-4-1">3.4.1</a>). Style-infused variants of the training images are generated under a composite objective of style, content, and adversarial losses, mirroring AdvCam. The training objective combines the standard cross-entropy loss $L_{\text{ce}}$ with a contrastive margin loss $L_{\text{m}}$. This margin loss minimizes the feature distance between the clean image and its adversarial counterpart. At the same time, it maximizes the distance to the style-source image. Treating the source image as a hard negative explicitly increases inter-class margins in feature space. This improves both robustness and generalization. SAT constrains perturbations within an $\ell_\infty$-norm ball and therefore does not strictly produce UAEs. However, its style-fusion-based adversarial example generation aligns closely with the other methods in this category. This makes it worth discussing here.

<strong>Attack-side: style as a transferability tool.</strong> StyLess <a class="cite" href="#ref-style-augmentation-styless">Liang &amp; Xiao (2023)</a> and STM <a class="cite" href="#ref-style-augmentation-STM">Ge et al. (2023)</a> use style augmentation to improve the transferability of $\ell_p$-bounded attacks. Both are motivated by the same observation. Conventional attacks overfit to the style-sensitive representations of the surrogate model. The two methods differ in where they inject style variation. StyLess <a class="cite" href="#ref-style-augmentation-styless">Liang &amp; Xiao (2023)</a> augments the surrogate model itself. It adds adaptive instance normalization layers and injects synthesized style features. This produces multiple stylistic variants of the surrogate that share content but differ in feature statistics. Jointly optimizing across these variants encourages the perturbation to depend on robust content features rather than fragile, model-specific style cues. STM <a class="cite" href="#ref-style-augmentation-STM">Ge et al. (2023)</a> operates in input space. It stylizes each input through an arbitrary style transfer network, with a style embedding sampled from a standard orthogonal distribution vector. It then blends the stylized image with the original under a content-preserving mixing ratio before computing gradients. STM’s mechanism mirrors the blind-spot attack <a class="cite" href="#ref-color-blind-spot-iclr-2019">Zhang et al. (2019b)</a> (Section <a href="#section-3-1-1">3.1.1</a>). The style-transferred image itself is not adversarial. Instead, it exposes the surrogate to cross-domain variations that lie in blind spots of its decision boundaries. Neither method strictly qualifies as a UAE, since both stay within $\ell_p$ bounds. However, together they reveal how strongly style features drive adversarial vulnerability. This validates style-aware attack design even within the conventional pixel-bounded threat model.

<strong>Summary:</strong> Style augmentation uses style transformations as a tool. It trains robust models for defense and improves transferability for attack. The defense methods (Robey et al., CVAE, AdvMix, SAT) generate UAE-like training samples. They represent some of the earliest defenses against style-transfer-based UAEs. The attack methods (StyLess, STM) and SAT itself sit just outside the strict UAE definition because of $\ell_p$ bounds. Yet all of them reinforce the same observation. Style features are a substantial axis of model vulnerability. This holds whether style is exploited directly, as in style fusion or style perturbation UAEs, or indirectly, as a way to improve the transferability of pixel-bounded attacks.

<h4 id="section-3-4-4">3.4.4 Content Editing</h4>

The previous three subcategories perturb low-level visual style. Content editing-based UAEs instead modify high-level semantic content such as facial attributes, background objects, and scene composition. They do so while preserving the overall semantic interpretation of the image. These attacks rely on generative models with disentangled or controllable latent spaces. This enables targeted modifications without compromising photorealism. The methods trace a clear progression along two dimensions. The first is the generative backbone, moving from attribute-conditional GANs to StyleGAN to diffusion models. The second is the form of control, moving from attribute vectors to feature interpolation to text prompts to language instructions.

<strong>Attribute-space editing on GAN backbones.</strong> The first content editing-based UAEs target face recognition through GAN-based attribute manipulation. Parametric Adversarial Attack <a class="cite" href="#ref-content-editing-parametric-transformation">Joshi et al. (2019)</a> treats the attribute vectors of conditional generative models as the perturbation space. Examples of such models include Fader Networks <a class="cite" href="#ref-fader-network">Lample et al. (2017)</a> and AttributeGANs <a class="cite" href="#ref-attgan">He et al. (2019)</a>. It performs gradient-based optimization in attribute space rather than pixel space. SemanticAdv <a class="cite" href="#ref-content-editing-semanticadv">Qiu et al. (2020)</a> refines this approach. It combines StarGAN-based <a class="cite" href="#ref-choi2018stargan">Choi et al. (2018)</a> attribute editing with feature-map interpolation between the original and edited images. This is guided by an adversarial loss and a smoothness loss. The interpolation-based formulation provides finer control and better transferability. It establishes the paradigm’s core principle: optimize within a disentangled semantic space.

<strong>Text-guided latent editing.</strong> CLIP2Protect <a class="cite" href="#ref-content-ediging-clip2protect">Shamshad et al. (2023)</a> brings natural-language control to the attack. The input face is inverted into the StyleGAN <a class="cite" href="#ref-styleGAN">Karras et al. (2019)</a> latent space. It is then optimized under three losses. The first is a directional CLIP loss that encodes the text instruction, such as a specific adversarial makeup style. The second is an adversarial loss. The third is an identity-preservation loss. Text guidance offers flexible, human-interpretable control over the edit direction. CLIP’s joint vision-language embedding supplies a perceptual constraint that keeps the result photorealistic.

<strong>Diffusion-based content editing.</strong> The shift from GANs to diffusion brings stronger generative priors and more structured editing. SemDiff <a class="cite" href="#ref-content-editing-semdiff">Dai et al. (2025)</a> edits semantic facial attributes such as smiling, brow shape, and jawline. It does so within the intermediate noisy latent space of diffusion models. It combines adversarial loss with CLIP-based semantic alignment. It also uses learnable attribute weights for fine-grained control. ACA <a class="cite" href="#ref-content-editding-ACA">Chen et al. (2023b)</a> generalizes beyond faces. It projects the input onto the natural image manifold through DDIM inversion <a class="cite" href="#ref-ddim">Song et al. (2021)</a>. It then optimizes within this low-dimensional latent space. This enables photorealistic modifications to shape, texture, and color. Operating on a learned manifold inherently constrains outputs to plausible imagery. This is what improves both perceptual quality and black-box transferability.

<strong>Language-instructed diffusion editing.</strong> Instruct2Attack (I2A) <a class="cite" href="#ref-content-editing-instruct2attack">Liu et al. (2023b)</a> combines language-guided control with diffusion in a fully automated pipeline. It generates a semantic editing instruction automatically. BLIP-2 <a class="cite" href="#ref-blip2">Li et al. (2023)</a> captions the image, and GPT-4 <a class="cite" href="#ref-gpt4">Achiam et al. (2023)</a> produces an adversarial instruction such as “add bamboo in background”. It then guides the reverse diffusion process by adversarially modulating both image and text features. The perturbation lies at the semantic level rather than as removable pixel noise. Because of this, I2A resists diffusion-based purification defenses such as DiffPure <a class="cite" href="#ref-diffpure">Nie et al. (2022)</a>, which target removable noise.

<strong>Summary:</strong> Content editing methods evolve along two dimensions: generative backbone and form of control. The progression runs from attribute-space gradient attacks (Parametric, SemanticAdv), to text-guided StyleGAN editing (CLIP2Protect), to diffusion-based attribute and content editing (SemDiff, ACA), to language-instructed diffusion attacks (I2A). Operating in a generative model’s structured latent space yields three benefits. The perturbations stay imperceptible, since large pixel changes blend into natural image statistics. Each edit direction carries a semantic meaning, which makes them interpretable. They also resist defenses that target removable pixel noise.

<h4 id="section-3-4-5">3.4.5 Overview of Style Transfer and Content Editing-Based UAEs</h4>

The four subcategories are style fusion, style perturbation, style augmentation, and content editing. Across all of them, a consistent design principle emerges. These methods target structured semantic axes rather than raw pixel statistics. This produces perturbations that look more natural and transfer better than $\ell_p$-bounded noise. The methods also trace a clear progression. Early methods rely on hand-crafted operations, such as Gram-matrix style transfer (tAdv) and feature-statistic perturbation (FSA). Later methods use GAN-based attribute editing (Parametric Adversarial Attack, SemanticAdv). The most recent methods use diffusion- and language-driven content modification (SemDiff, ACA, I2A). Each generation introduces finer control and stronger generative priors. Pixel-space adversarial training generalizes poorly to perturbations along these semantic axes. This motivates defenses that operate in feature, style, or content spaces. The style augmentation defense methods (Robey et al., CVAE, AdvMix, SAT) are designed to fill exactly this role.

<h3 id="section-3-5">3.5 Generative Model-Based UAEs</h3>

Generative model-based UAEs use GANs, VAEs, normalizing flows, and diffusion models to synthesize adversarial examples. The methods split along two axes. The first axis is whether the attack is conditioned on a reference image. Some methods synthesize UAEs from random noise. Others perturb a given input within a generative model’s latent space. The second axis is whether the generator outputs a full image or only an adversarial perturbation. The methods are organized mainly by the first axis. A related class of generative perturbation synthesis predates the formal UAE concept and remains $\ell_p$-bounded. It is included at the end for historical and methodological continuity.

<h4 id="section-3-5-1">3.5.1 Generating UAE from Scratch</h4>

<figure id="figure-9"><a class="figure-link" href="/blog/beyond-the-lp-ball/figure-9.webp"><img src="/blog/beyond-the-lp-ball/figure-9.webp" alt="Six adversarial images generated from scratch by GAN- and diffusion-based methods." width="2000" height="474" loading="lazy" decoding="async"></a><figcaption><strong>Figure 9:</strong> Examples of generative model-based UAEs synthesized <em>from scratch</em> (Section <a href="#section-3-5-1">3.5.1</a>). The images are sampled directly from a generative prior (named under each image) and conditioned on the ground-truth label, which acts as a content anchor. Images are from the cited works.</figcaption></figure>

Methods in this group sample UAEs from a generator’s prior with no reference image. The synthesized image must carry semantic content that matches the ground-truth label. It must also cause the target model to misclassify. Figure <a href="#figure-9">9</a> shows examples of UAEs synthesized directly from random noise by representative methods in this category.

<strong>Defining the paradigm.</strong> Song et al. <a class="cite" href="#ref-uae_origin">Song et al. (2018)</a> introduced the concept of UAEs and proposed the first generation method. It is built around an Auxiliary Classifier GAN (AC-GAN) <a class="cite" href="#ref-acgan">Odena et al. (2017)</a>. The attack searches the AC-GAN’s latent space for a noise vector that minimizes a composite objective of three terms. The first term is an adversarial loss $L_0$ that induces target misclassification. The second is a proximity loss $L_1$ that ties the optimized latent to a randomly sampled initialization. The third is a classification consistency loss $L_2$. This loss uses the AC-GAN’s auxiliary classifier to lock the generated image to its ground-truth class, which mitigates label flipping. The attack uses no explicit $\ell_p$ budget. As a result, it produces images with much greater perceptual realism than heavily perturbed $\ell_p$-bounded examples at comparable attack success rates. It also proves effective against both adversarial training and certified defenses calibrated for traditional attacks.

<strong>Sampling adversarial distributions directly.</strong> AT-GAN <a class="cite" href="#ref-genmodel-scratch-atgan">Wang et al. (2019)</a> shifts from per-instance latent search to learning the full adversarial distribution. A standard GAN is first trained to approximate the natural image distribution. Its pretrained generator is then fine-tuned into an attack generator under two objectives. The first is to stay close to the original generator’s outputs. The second is to induce the target classifier to misclassify generated images into a specified class. Once trained, the attack generator produces UAEs directly from random noise. It needs no further gradient-based optimization. This offers an efficient alternative to the iterative latent search of Song et al. <a class="cite" href="#ref-uae_origin">Song et al. (2018)</a>.

<strong>Diffusion-based refinements.</strong> GAN backbones are next replaced by diffusion models. Diffusion models offer greater training stability and sample diversity. AdvDiffuser <a class="cite" href="#ref-advdiffuser">Chen et al. (2023a)</a> perturbs the noisy latent of the diffusion model during reverse diffusion using the conventional adversarial attack algorithm PGD <a class="cite" href="#ref-madry_at">Madry et al. (2018)</a>. Notably, AdvDiffuser supports NAE generation from both random noise and a reference image. AdvDiff <a class="cite" href="#ref-advdiff">Dai et al. (2024)</a> integrates adversarial guidance directly into the reverse diffusion process of a pretrained conditional diffusion model. It needs no additional training. At each denoising step, it injects the gradient of the target label’s log-likelihood with respect to the noisy intermediate state. At initialization, it perturbs the sampled noise with an adversarial prior derived from the target classifier’s log-probability gradient. AdvDiff also shows stronger image fidelity and attack success rates than prior GAN-based UAEs. SD-NAE <a class="cite" href="#ref-sd-nae">Lin et al. (2024a)</a> reroutes the perturbation entirely. Rather than altering the latent code during reverse diffusion, it perturbs the embeddings of the class-relevant text prompt under adversarial optimization from the target classifier. Latent perturbation distorts generated images and suffers gradient attenuation across the iterative denoising steps. Text-embedding optimization avoids both problems. However, it remains computationally expensive and still yields a substantial fraction of label-flipped outputs.

<strong>Addressing label flipping.</strong> VENOM <a class="cite" href="#ref-venom">Kuurila-Zhang et al. (2025)</a> confronts the persistent label-flipping problem that prior generative UAE methods report only qualitatively. It contributes a momentum-based adaptive control strategy for adversarial guidance during conditional sampling. The strength of the injected guidance is modulated step by step according to the state of the intermediate noisy latent. This achieves near-perfect alignment between the semantic content of generated UAEs and their ground-truth labels. It also produces higher image-quality scores among non-reference-image UAE methods. Adversarial guidance in diffusion models is unstable when injected uniformly. The denoising process is highly sensitive to perturbations. Only carefully tuned, state-aware guidance avoids distorting the underlying generative prior.

<strong>Summary:</strong> From Song et al. to VENOM, this line of work refines two questions together. The first is how to sample adversarial examples from a generative prior. The second is how to keep those samples semantically faithful. The methods shift in architecture, from AC-GAN to dedicated attack generators to diffusion models. They also shift in procedure, from latent search to distribution learning to conditional guidance to adaptive control. These shifts steadily close the gap between attack strength and perceptual fidelity. VENOM’s lesson is direct. As generative priors grow more powerful, adversarial signals must be injected more carefully. Otherwise, they disrupt the very priors that make UAEs realistic.

<h4 id="section-3-5-2">3.5.2 Generating UAE from Reference Image</h4>

<figure id="figure-10"><a class="figure-link" href="/blog/beyond-the-lp-ball/figure-10.webp"><img src="/blog/beyond-the-lp-ball/figure-10.webp" alt="Clean and adversarial image pairs from GAN-based, flow-based and diffusion-based UAE methods that start from a reference image." width="2000" height="672" loading="lazy" decoding="async"></a><figcaption><strong>Figure 10:</strong> Examples of generative model-based UAEs generated <em>from a reference image</em> (Section <a href="#section-3-5-2">3.5.2</a>). The top row shows clean images and the bottom row the corresponding adversarial images, with the generating method named underneath. The panels group the methods by generative backbone. Images are from the cited works.</figcaption></figure>

A second class of generative UAE methods conditions on a given input image. These methods perturb the image within the latent space of a generative model. The reference image provides a content anchor. The latent space provides a structured perturbation manifold. Adversarial signals along this manifold tend to stay on or near the natural image manifold. We organize methods by their generative backbone. The backbone constrains the form of the latent space and the optimization strategies available. The latent space is continuous and approximate for VAEs and GANs, exact and invertible for normalizing flows, and sequential and noisy for diffusion models. A recurring duality appears across all three backbones. Latent-space perturbation fools models, while latent-space adversarial training improves robustness without sacrificing accuracy. Figure <a href="#figure-10">10</a> shows one clean/adversarial pair per method, grouped by generative backbone.

<strong>VAE- and GAN-Based Methods</strong>

Early generative UAEs (2018 to 2022) relied on VAEs and GANs, often in combination. They explored several axes in parallel. These axes are latent-space search, feature-encoder inputs, physical realizability, and on-manifold adversarial training.

<strong>Pioneering latent-space search.</strong> Zhao et al. <a class="cite" href="#ref-genmodel-reference-GNAE">Zhao et al. (2018)</a> introduced one of the earliest generative model-based UAE methods. Their framework couples an inverter with a generator. The inverter maps reference data (images or text) to a dense continuous latent representation. The generator maps Gaussian noise vectors back to data samples. The inverter minimizes two quantities. The first is the reconstruction error between a reference sample and its generated counterpart. The second is the divergence between the sampled noise vector and the encoded latent. A search algorithm then explores the neighborhood of the encoded latent. It looks for one whose generator output is adversarial. The method handles both continuous (image) and discrete (text) domains. It requires no classifier gradients. It also applies to black-box models. Therefore, it establishes semantic latent spaces as a search space for interpretable adversarial examples.

<strong>Feature-level perturbation and physical realizability.</strong> AdvGAN++ <a class="cite" href="#ref-genmodel-reference-advgan++">Jandial et al. (2019)</a> extends AdvGAN <a class="cite" href="#ref-generating-perturbations-ijcai">Xiao et al. (2018a)</a>. AdvGAN is a GAN-based generator of $\ell_2$-bounded perturbations. AdvGAN++ replaces the GAN’s raw-image input with latent feature maps extracted by a separate encoder. The generator then learns to map these features to adversarial images. It does so under a composite objective of GAN, adversarial, and $\ell_2$-perturbation losses. Perturbing latent features yields higher attack success than perturbing raw inputs. This agrees with the hypothesis that latent representations are more vulnerable to adversarial manipulation than pixel space. Adversarial Generative Nets (AGNs) <a class="cite" href="#ref-genmodel-reference-eyeglasses">Sharif et al. (2019)</a> move this paradigm into the physical world. A GAN synthesizes adversarial artifacts, notably eyeglasses. It uses a GAN loss to enforce similarity to real eyeglass references. It uses an adversarial loss to induce face-recognition errors. AGNs treat adversarial artifacts as learnable, physically realizable objects. This connects digital adversarial examples and real-world deployment.

<strong>Black-box and on-manifold latent attacks.</strong> Latent-HSJA <a class="cite" href="#ref-genmodel-reference-blackboxeccv">Na et al. (2022)</a> adapts the HopSkipJump-Attack (HSJA) <a class="cite" href="#ref-hsja">Chen et al. (2020)</a> to a GAN’s latent space. It perturbs the latent vector of a target image toward that of a source image under an $\ell_2$ constraint. This constraint is imposed in latent space rather than image space. The budget governs semantic proximity rather than pixel proximity. Therefore, the resulting attack remains a valid UAE in image space. It also requires far fewer queries than pixel-space black-box methods. Two complementary works take the defense angle. Stutz et al. <a class="cite" href="#ref-genmodel-reference-disentangle">Stutz et al. (2019)</a> formalize on-manifold adversarial examples. They use VAE-GANs <a class="cite" href="#ref-VAE-GAN">Larsen et al. (2016)</a> to approximate the data manifold. Through on-manifold adversarial training, they show that robustness and generalization can be achieved at the same time. This challenges the prevailing assumption of an inherent trade-off. Dual Manifold Adversarial Training (DMAT) <a class="cite" href="#ref-genmodel-reference-dual">Lin et al. (2020)</a> extends this. It trains on both off-manifold and on-manifold adversarial samples. The resulting models resist $\ell_p$-bounded and UAE attacks jointly. They also further narrow the robustness-generalization gap <a class="cite" href="#ref-robust-generalization-gap">Schmidt et al. (2018)</a>; <a class="cite" href="#ref-robust-generalizatioin-gap2">Rice et al. (2020)</a>; <a class="cite" href="#ref-awp">Wu et al. (2020)</a>.

<strong>Normalizing-Flow-Based Methods</strong>

A short follow-up line (2021 to 2023) exploits the exact invertibility of normalizing flows <a class="cite" href="#ref-nice-normalizing-flows">Dinh et al. (2014)</a>; <a class="cite" href="#ref-glow">Kingma &amp; Dhariwal (2018)</a>. This invertibility yields precise latent representations of images. It contrasts with the approximate encoder-decoder mappings of VAEs and GANs. Both representative methods are built on Glow <a class="cite" href="#ref-glow">Kingma &amp; Dhariwal (2018)</a>. Adversarial Latent Attacks (A-LA) <a class="cite" href="#ref-genmodel-reference-nfla">Yüksel et al. (2021)</a> runs PGD-style gradient-based optimization directly in the exact latent space. This produces on-manifold adversarial examples. A-LA is reused as a data augmentation method during adversarial training. In this role, it reinforces the on-manifold robustness findings of Stutz et al. <a class="cite" href="#ref-genmodel-reference-disentangle">Stutz et al. (2019)</a> and DMAT <a class="cite" href="#ref-genmodel-reference-dual">Lin et al. (2020)</a>. Joint Space Attack (JSA) <a class="cite" href="#ref-genmodel-reference-IJSAT">Lau et al. (2023)</a> extends the idea. It jointly optimizes perturbations in both latent and image spaces. This yields a better balance between standard and robust accuracy. It also further narrows the robustness-generalization gap. Both methods are also designed as data augmentation techniques for adversarial training.

<strong>Diffusion-Based Methods</strong>

From 2023 onward, diffusion models dominate reference-image UAE generation. These models inherit the perceptual quality of modern image generators. The design space expands progressively. It moves from latent perturbation under transfer-friendly objectives, to text-embedding and mask-based control, and most recently to conditioning on a learned concept distribution rather than a single reference image.

<strong>Latent-space attacks.</strong> DiffAttack <a class="cite" href="#ref-diffattack">Chen et al. (2024)</a> uses DDIM inversion <a class="cite" href="#ref-ddim">Song et al. (2021)</a> to project images into the diffusion latent space. It optimizes under a combined adversarial, structural, and transferability objective. The diffusion model serves as an implicit surrogate. The transfer loss boosts cross-model transferability. The denoising trajectory and structural loss suppress high-frequency artifacts. Prior methods could secure either imperceptibility or transferability but not both. DiffAttack secures both at once. AdvDiffuser <a class="cite" href="#ref-advdiffuser">Chen et al. (2023a)</a> integrates PGD attacks <a class="cite" href="#ref-madry_at">Madry et al. (2018)</a> into intermediate noisy states of the reverse diffusion process. It also introduces adversarial inpainting guided by GradCam <a class="cite" href="#ref-gradcam">Selvaraju et al. (2017)</a>. Salient object regions receive weaker perturbations to preserve semantics. AdvDiffuser is one of the earliest diffusion-based UAE methods. It establishes that diffusion offers a more powerful generative substrate than GANs for high-quality UAE synthesis.

<strong>Diffusion-enhanced PGD.</strong> DiffPGD <a class="cite" href="#ref-diffpgd">Xue et al. (2023)</a> pairs traditional PGD <a class="cite" href="#ref-madry_at">Madry et al. (2018)</a> with diffusion-based denoising. At each optimization step, the adversarial example undergoes a full forward and reverse diffusion pass. Gradients are then computed on the purified intermediate. This design produces naturalistic adversarial examples. They also resist diffusion-based purification defenses such as DiffPure <a class="cite" href="#ref-diffpure">Nie et al. (2022)</a>. <strong>DiffPGD remains constrained by an $\ell_p$ budget and does not strictly qualify as a UAE</strong>. However, it is closely related to diffusion-based UAE generation, so we include it here.

<strong>Text-embedding and mask-based control.</strong> Prompt2Perturb (P2P) <a class="cite" href="#ref-genmodel-diff-p2p">Medghalchi et al. (2025)</a> builds on SD-NAE <a class="cite" href="#ref-sd-nae">Lin et al. (2024a)</a>. It adapts text-embedding optimization to the medical imaging domain. It adds a mean squared error loss that preserves tissue homogeneity and structural details in breast ultrasound images. This shows that domain-specific constraints make text-conditioned diffusion UAEs portable across application areas. Two further methods, Semantic Transformation (ST) and Latent Masking (LM) <a class="cite" href="#ref-genmodel-diff-ST-LM">Wang et al. (2023a)</a>, decouple the cost-quality trade-off in diffusion UAE generation. ST perturbs the image latent while fine-tuning the diffusion model under adversarial and LPIPS <a class="cite" href="#ref-lpips">Zhang et al. (2018)</a> losses. It achieves strong attacks at high computational cost. LM constructs a latent-space mask via GradCam <a class="cite" href="#ref-gradcam">Selvaraju et al. (2017)</a> and saliency maps <a class="cite" href="#ref-saliency-map1">Simonyan et al. (2013)</a>; <a class="cite" href="#ref-saliency-map2">Srinivas &amp; Fleuret (2019)</a>. It then combines the latent representations of the source and an auxiliary reference image without fine-tuning the model. This trades some attack strength for substantial efficiency.

<strong>Concept-distribution conditioning.</strong> ConceptAdv <a class="cite" href="#ref-genmodel-diff-concept">Zhang et al. (2026)</a> replaces the single fixed reference image with a distribution over semantically equivalent images. A <em>concept</em> is a probability distribution over images sharing the same semantic identity (e.g., multiple views of the same object or subject). Adversarial examples are sampled from a product of two distributions. The first is a victim distribution that rewards target misclassification. The second is a concept distribution that constrains outputs to remain within the underlying concept. Compared with single-reference latent-space baselines such as DiffAttack <a class="cite" href="#ref-diffattack">Chen et al. (2024)</a> and ACA <a class="cite" href="#ref-content-editding-ACA">Chen et al. (2023b)</a>, this distributional formulation yields substantially higher white-box attack success and black-box transferability.

<strong>Summary:</strong> Reference-image UAE generation follows a clear backbone progression. It moves from VAEs and GANs, to normalizing flows, to diffusion models. Attack and defense also recur as a duality within each backbone. On the attack side, latent-space search yields adversarial examples. On the defense side, the same search yields on-manifold robustness gains. Control precision also increases throughout. Early methods use latent-vector search (Zhao et al., AdvGAN++, Latent-HSJA). Later methods use exact-latent gradient optimization (A-LA, JSA). Diffusion-based methods use fine-grained text-embedding and masking strategies (P2P, ST, LM). The most recent step replaces the single reference image with a learned concept distribution (ConceptAdv). As generative priors grow stronger, generative UAE methods inherit their expressivity. They also exploit their structure for both adversarial and defensive ends.

<h4 id="section-3-5-3">3.5.3 Generating Perturbations</h4>

A small but historically important group uses generative models to synthesize adversarial perturbations rather than full adversarial examples. <strong>These methods remain constrained by an $\ell_p$ budget and do not strictly qualify as UAEs</strong>. However their direct methodological lineage with the generative UAE methods above makes them worth including here. AdvGAN <a class="cite" href="#ref-generating-perturbations-ijcai">Xiao et al. (2018a)</a> trains a GAN under three losses. An adversarial loss induces target misclassification. A GAN loss preserves image fidelity. A hinge loss enforces an $\ell_2$ norm constraint. This produces a generator that maps inputs to norm-bounded perturbations. Generative Adversarial Perturbation (GAP) <a class="cite" href="#ref-generating-perturbations-cvpr">Poursaeed et al. (2018)</a> uses U-Net <a class="cite" href="#ref-unet">Ronneberger et al. (2015)</a> or ResNet generators <a class="cite" href="#ref-resnetgenerator">Johnson et al. (2016)</a> to synthesize a range of perturbation variants. These variants are both universal and image-dependent, and both targeted and untargeted. Both methods predate the formal UAE concept. They were among the earliest demonstrations that generative models could synthesize adversarial perturbations more imperceptibly and more efficiently than direct optimization. AdvGAN++ <a class="cite" href="#ref-genmodel-reference-advgan++">Jandial et al. (2019)</a>, discussed earlier, extends AdvGAN into the UAE regime. It relocates the perturbation from the pixel to latent feature space.

<h4 id="section-3-5-4">3.5.4 Overview of Generative Model-Based UAEs</h4>

Generative model-based UAEs follow the evolution of modern generative modeling itself. The progression runs from AC-GANs (Song et al.), through VAE-GAN hybrids and GAN-based latent attacks (Zhao et al., Stutz et al., DMAT, AGNs, AdvGAN++, Latent-HSJA), to normalizing flows (A-LA, JSA), and then to diffusion-based methods (AdvDiff, SD-NAE, VENOM, DiffAttack, AdvDiffuser, P2P, ST, LM, ConceptAdv). Adversarial signals injected into a generator’s latent space inherit the generator’s natural image prior. The resulting UAEs lie on or near the natural image manifold. They remain perceptually plausible even at large pixel-space distortion. Attack and defense are also deeply coupled within each backbone. Latent-space attacks and on-manifold adversarial training are two uses of the same mechanism. Advances on either side tend to inform the other. Adversarial training schemes operating in pixel space generalize poorly to generative-model-based attacks. Defenses operating in the latent space of a comparable generative model are the more natural counterpart. The on-manifold and dual-manifold training results above illustrate this.

<h2 id="section-4">4 Defenses Against Unrestricted Adversarial Examples</h2>

Threat models have always shaped the design of the defense methods. Almost every widely deployed defense was built around a single assumption. The adversary is constrained to a small $\ell_p$ ball in pixel space. This holds for input preprocessing <a class="cite" href="#ref-defense-jpeg-compression">Das et al. (2017)</a>; <a class="cite" href="#ref-defense-feature-squeezing">Xu et al. (2018)</a>; <a class="cite" href="#ref-defense-meanblur">Li &amp; Li (2017)</a>, certified verification <a class="cite" href="#ref-certified-defense-crown">Zhang et al. (2020)</a>; <a class="cite" href="#ref-rs">Cohen et al. (2019)</a>, and adversarial training <a class="cite" href="#ref-madry_at">Madry et al. (2018)</a>; <a class="cite" href="#ref-awp">Wu et al. (2020)</a>. UAEs break that assumption by construction. The consequences run through the entire defense landscape. We first examine why defenses built for $\ell_p$ norm-bounded attacks do not transfer to UAEs (Section <a href="#section-4-1">4.1</a>). We then survey defenses proposed specifically against them (Section <a href="#section-4-2">4.2</a>). Both analyses point to the same problem. UAEs span a varied space of perturbation types. Existing defenses each cover only a small part of this space.

<h3 id="section-4-1">4.1 Defense Failure Modes Under Unrestricted Adversarial Examples</h3>

The dominant defenses against norm-bounded attacks are input purification, certification, and adversarial training. They are designed with the same assumption about the adversarial perturbation. It is a small, high-frequency, pixel-level perturbation laid on top of an otherwise clean image. UAEs break that assumption from three angles. Each one fails differently.

<strong>Preprocessing and purification defenses are tuned to the wrong signal.</strong> A large family of inference-time defenses tries to strip out the adversarial perturbation before classification. These include bit-depth reduction and feature squeezing <a class="cite" href="#ref-defense-feature-squeezing">Xu et al. (2018)</a>, mean or median smoothing <a class="cite" href="#ref-defense-meanblur">Li &amp; Li (2017)</a>, JPEG compression <a class="cite" href="#ref-defense-jpeg-compression">Das et al. (2017)</a>, self-supervised purifiers <a class="cite" href="#ref-nrp">Naseer et al. (2020)</a>, and diffusion-based purifiers like DiffPure <a class="cite" href="#ref-diffpure">Nie et al. (2022)</a>. They share the same logic. An $\ell_p$-bounded perturbation leaves a small, usually high-frequency residual that is statistically separable from natural image content. Bandlimiting in the frequency domain or reducing the pixel bit-depth of the image should therefore preserve the semantic signal while removing the adversarial noise. UAEs break this premise. Transformation-based attacks recast the perturbation as a low-frequency, on-manifold change. ReColorAdv <a class="cite" href="#ref-color-functional-attack-nips-2019">Laidlaw &amp; Feizi (2019)</a> applies a smooth, globally consistent color mapping. ColorFool <a class="cite" href="#ref-color-colorfool-cvpr-2020">Shamsabadi et al. (2020b)</a> and Natural ColorFool <a class="cite" href="#ref-color-natural-colorfool-nips-2022">Yuan et al. (2022)</a> recolor whole semantic regions while staying inside natural color statistics. Learned-filter attacks like EdgeFool <a class="cite" href="#ref-filter-edgefool">Shamsabadi et al. (2020a)</a>, ACE <a class="cite" href="#ref-filter-color-ace">Zhao et al. (2020b)</a>, and FilterFool <a class="cite" href="#ref-filter-learnable">Shamsabadi et al. (2021)</a> hide the adversarial signal inside ordinary image-enhancement operations. Style transfer and content-editing methods such as AdvCam <a class="cite" href="#ref-style-fusion-adversarial-camouflage">Duan et al. (2020)</a>, ACA <a class="cite" href="#ref-content-editding-ACA">Chen et al. (2023b)</a>, and Instruct2Attack <a class="cite" href="#ref-content-editing-instruct2attack">Liu et al. (2023b)</a> move the perturbation entirely into the semantic or generative latent space. Quantization and smoothing do not affect these kinds of adversarial signals. Under the statistics purifiers assume, they are natural image content. The failure is conceptual. cAdv <a class="cite" href="#ref-color-semantic-manipulation-iclr-2020">Bhattad et al. (2020)</a> survives JPEG compression <a class="cite" href="#ref-defense-jpeg-compression">Das et al. (2017)</a> and feature squeezing <a class="cite" href="#ref-defense-feature-squeezing">Xu et al. (2018)</a>. Instruct2Attack <a class="cite" href="#ref-content-editing-instruct2attack">Liu et al. (2023b)</a>, DiffAttack <a class="cite" href="#ref-diffattack">Chen et al. (2024)</a>, and VENOM <a class="cite" href="#ref-venom">Kuurila-Zhang et al. (2025)</a> remain adversarial after DiffPure <a class="cite" href="#ref-diffpure">Nie et al. (2022)</a> because its perturbation lives at the semantic level rather than as removable pixel noise. Once the adversarial signal sits on the natural image manifold, a defense built to push the input back onto that manifold cannot work.

<strong>Certified defenses guarantee only what their constraint set contains.</strong> Certified or verified robustness provides a provable guarantee, but the guarantee is indexed to the set of perturbations the certificate is written over, and the certificates in widespread use are written over $\ell_p$ balls. Interval Bound Propagation (IBP) and related verification methods <a class="cite" href="#ref-certified-defense-crown">Zhang et al. (2020)</a> certify robustness inside an $\ell_\infty$ ball. Randomized smoothing <a class="cite" href="#ref-rs">Cohen et al. (2019)</a> certifies robustness inside an $\ell_2$ ball by averaging predictions over Gaussian noise. Each certificate is only mathematically valid within the $\ell_p$-norm-bound. UAEs are built to operate outside that bound while still looking natural. Shadow Attack <a class="cite" href="#ref-distance-metric-shaddow-attack">Ghiasi et al. (2020)</a> makes this explicit. It augments PGD with perceptual regularizers (total variation, mean color, inter-channel consistency). This produces perturbations whose $\ell_p$ norm is large enough to escape the certified radius but whose appearance stays plausible. It breaks IBP-based <a class="cite" href="#ref-certified-defense-crown">Zhang et al. (2020)</a> and randomized-smoothing-based <a class="cite" href="#ref-rs">Cohen et al. (2019)</a> certifications alike. Non-targeted UAE methods produce the same effect almost as a side consequence. The original AC-GAN-based UAE generator of Song et al. <a class="cite" href="#ref-uae_origin">Song et al. (2018)</a> bypasses certified defenses because the synthesized image is not built by perturbing any specific clean input at all. Most recent diffusion-based UAE methods, such as VENOM <a class="cite" href="#ref-venom">Kuurila-Zhang et al. (2025)</a>, AdvDiff <a class="cite" href="#ref-advdiff">Dai et al. (2024)</a>, and DiffAttack <a class="cite" href="#ref-diffattack">Chen et al. (2024)</a>, have been tested against randomized smoothing <a class="cite" href="#ref-rs">Cohen et al. (2019)</a> and still reach high attack success rates. An $\ell_p$ certificate is not a general statement about adversarial robustness. It is a statement about robustness within one particular geometric region, and the UAE threat model is simply beyond that region.

<strong>Certification beyond the $\ell_p$ ball, and where it stops.</strong> Some certified defenses are built beyond the $\ell_p$ ball to defend against certain types of UAEs. This family of works extends certification from the $\ell_p$ norm ball to parameterized transformations instead. DeepG <a class="cite" href="#ref-certified-semantic-deepg">Balunovic et al. (2019)</a> certifies robustness against compositions of geometric transformations, covering rotation, translation, scaling, shearing, brightness, and contrast. Semantify-NN <a class="cite" href="#ref-certified-semantic-semantifynn">Mohapatra et al. (2020)</a> inserts semantic perturbation layers in front of the network, so that any existing $\ell_p$ verifier can certify hue, saturation, lightness, brightness, contrast, rotation, translation, and occlusion without modification. TSS <a class="cite" href="#ref-certified-semantic-tss">Li et al. (2021)</a> replaces the Gaussian noise of randomized smoothing <a class="cite" href="#ref-rs">Cohen et al. (2019)</a> with transformation-specific noise and trains the smoothed classifier, producing certified accuracy on geometric transformations. What these certificates cover is the photometric and geometric subcategories of our taxonomy (Sections <a href="#section-3-1-2">3.1.2</a> and <a href="#section-3-1-4">3.1.4</a>), considerably more than an $\ell_p$ ball. Their reach ends where the parameterization does. All three need the perturbation family written analytically and with few parameters, since the certificate is obtained by relaxing or sampling that parameter space at a cost that grows with its dimension. Style, content-editing, and generative UAEs are not parameterized attacks. Their perturbation is the forward pass of a generator. Certification against these types of UAEs is therefore not blocked by the $\ell_p$ ball, but by the absence of a compact analytic description of what those categories do to an image.

<strong>Adversarial training generalizes only within its own threat model.</strong> Adversarial training <a class="cite" href="#ref-madry_at">Madry et al. (2018)</a>; <a class="cite" href="#ref-fgsm">Goodfellow et al. (2015)</a>, including variants like TRADES <a class="cite" href="#ref-mi-trades2019">Zhang et al. (2019a)</a>, trains on PGD- or FGSM-style $\ell_p$-bounded examples. It confers robustness against attacks of the similar form. UAEs fall outside that form along two independent axes. In pixel space, UAE perturbations are typically far larger than the training $\ell_p$ budget. The spatial flow fields of stAdv <a class="cite" href="#ref-geometric-stadv">Xiao et al. (2018b)</a>, the camera-pose changes of ViewFool <a class="cite" href="#ref-geometric-viewfool">Dong et al. (2022)</a>, and the latent edits of ACA <a class="cite" href="#ref-content-editding-ACA">Chen et al. (2023b)</a> all produce pixel differences no $\ell_p$-trained model has seen during training. More fundamentally, UAEs occupy a different subspace entirely. Their perturbations are parameterized by semantic, stylistic, or geometric factors rather than pixel-wise additive noise. Even an arbitrarily large $\ell_p$ ball would therefore not contain them in a form the model could exploit. The empirical literature is consistent. stAdv <a class="cite" href="#ref-geometric-stadv">Xiao et al. (2018b)</a> reports that PGD-adversarially trained models remain vulnerable to spatial flow-field attacks. The blind-spot attack <a class="cite" href="#ref-color-blind-spot-iclr-2019">Zhang et al. (2019b)</a> shows that adversarially trained models fail on inputs displaced from the training manifold by nothing more than global contrast and brightness shifts. FSA <a class="cite" href="#ref-style-perturbation-feature-space">Xu et al. (2021)</a> finds that pixel-space adversarial training does not transfer to feature-space style perturbations. NPTM <a class="cite" href="#ref-distance-metric-lpips">Laidlaw et al. (2021)</a> confirms poor generalization to LPIPS-bounded attacks. $\ell_p$ adversarial training is robust to what it optimizes against. Its robustness does not transfer well to perturbations of other types.

The three failure modes point to the same conclusion. A defense only covers the constraint set it is built around, and the UAE threat model deliberately steps outside the constraint set. The failure is structural rather than a matter of tuning. Closing the gap requires defenses whose threat model is at least as broad as the perturbation space they aim to cover.

<h3 id="section-4-2">4.2 Defenses Trained by UAEs</h3>

The failures above all share one cause. Each defense is tied to the $\ell_p$ threat model, so they don’t transfer well to UAEs. A smaller but coherent body of work responds to this by dropping the $\ell_p$ assumption and building defenses around specific UAE families instead. They are grouped by mechanism rather than by the attack each accompanies. They fall into three categories. The first is on-manifold and latent-space adversarial training. The second is augmentation-based training that simulates UAE-like variations. The third is perceptual or stylistic adversarial training that replaces the $\ell_p$ constraint with a UAE-aligned one. Across all three, the approach is the same. They train against samples produced by a specific UAE mechanism. They then gain robustness to that mechanism and, in several cases, to nearby attack types as well.

<strong>Latent-space adversarial training on the natural image manifold.</strong> The most theoretically developed approach uses generative models to approximate the natural image manifold and perform adversarial training within it. Stutz et al. <a class="cite" href="#ref-genmodel-reference-disentangle">Stutz et al. (2019)</a> introduced this paradigm with VAE-GAN-based on-manifold adversarial examples. They show that on-manifold adversarial training boosts generalization, and that robustness and generalization are not necessarily in conflict. Dual Manifold Adversarial Training (DMAT) <a class="cite" href="#ref-genmodel-reference-dual">Lin et al. (2020)</a> extends this to defend against $\ell_p$-bounded and on-manifold attacks together. DMAT trains on both image-space and latent-space adversarial examples. It reaches robustness comparable to standard $\ell_p$ adversarial training against $\ell_\infty$ attacks. It also improves robustness to several attacks it did not train on, including color and filter-based corruptions and $\ell_2$ <a class="cite" href="#ref-szegedy2013intriguing">Szegedy et al. (2013)</a> and $\ell_1$ <a class="cite" href="#ref-L1-attack">Chen et al. (2018)</a> attacks. This narrows the robustness-generalization gap <a class="cite" href="#ref-robust-generalization-gap">Schmidt et al. (2018)</a>; <a class="cite" href="#ref-robust-generalizatioin-gap2">Rice et al. (2020)</a>; <a class="cite" href="#ref-awp">Wu et al. (2020)</a>. Subsequent work replaces the approximate VAE-GAN latent with the exact, invertible latent of normalizing flows. Adversarial Latent Attacks <a class="cite" href="#ref-genmodel-reference-nfla">Yüksel et al. (2021)</a> run PGD-style optimization in Glow’s <a class="cite" href="#ref-glow">Kingma &amp; Dhariwal (2018)</a> latent space and reuse the resulting on-manifold samples during training. Their goal is to improve generalization rather than adversarial robustness, and they report gains in standard test accuracy rather than robustness to any attack. The Joint Space Attack <a class="cite" href="#ref-genmodel-reference-IJSAT">Lau et al. (2023)</a> jointly perturbs latent and pixel spaces during training. It improves robustness to both seen $\ell_p$ attacks <a class="cite" href="#ref-fgsm">Goodfellow et al. (2015)</a>; <a class="cite" href="#ref-madry_at">Madry et al. (2018)</a>; <a class="cite" href="#ref-autoattack">Croce &amp; Hein (2020)</a> and unseen attacks such as Elastic, JEPG attack <a class="cite" href="#ref-elastic-attack">Kaufmann et al. (2019)</a>, and $\ell_2$ perturbations <a class="cite" href="#ref-szegedy2013intriguing">Szegedy et al. (2013)</a>. On-manifold adversarial training confers robustness when the manifold used at training time matches the one the attacker exploits at test time. The cross-family transfer these methods show is limited to color, filter, and $\ell_p$-type perturbations. None has been shown to generalize to geometric UAEs, style transfer, or diffusion-based content edits.

<strong>Augmentation against UAE-like natural variation.</strong> The second group uses the same idea. Rather than training against attacks crafted within a specific latent space, it augments the training set with UAE-like natural variations. MUNIT-based augmentation <a class="cite" href="#ref-style-augmentation-model-based">Robey et al. (2020)</a> disentangles content from a learned “nuisance space” of stylistic and environmental attributes and samples diverse variants from unpaired data. CVAE-based augmentation <a class="cite" href="#ref-style-augmentation-learning-perturbation-set">Wong &amp; Kolter (2021)</a> learns an $\ell_2$-constrained latent space from paired clean-perturbed data. Running PGD within that latent space turns it into a UAE generator reusable as a defensive data source covering blur, lighting changes, weather, and digital artifacts. AdvMix <a class="cite" href="#ref-style-augmentation-AdvMix">Gowal et al. (2020)</a> extends the same logic to StyleGAN <a class="cite" href="#ref-styleGAN">Karras et al. (2019)</a>. It disentangles content and style, perturbs each independently, and studies the interplay between adversarial training and disentangled augmentation. This group treats UAE defense as a special case of out-of-distribution generalization. Any natural variation an attacker might exploit should appear in some form during training. The limitation is coverage. The defense is robust to variations expressible in the chosen generative model. It fails on UAEs that exploit a different axis, such as geometric transformation or semantic content editing.

<strong>Stylistic and perceptual adversarial training.</strong> The third group replaces the $\ell_p$ ball with a constraint closer to the UAE the defense targets. Stylized Adversarial Training <a class="cite" href="#ref-style-augmentation-SAT">Naseer et al. (2022)</a> pairs style-fusion augmentation (analogous to AdvCam <a class="cite" href="#ref-style-fusion-adversarial-camouflage">Duan et al. (2020)</a>) with a max-margin contrastive loss that treats the style-source image as a hard negative, improving both robustness and generalization. Perceptual Adversarial Training (PAT), introduced alongside NPTM <a class="cite" href="#ref-distance-metric-lpips">Laidlaw et al. (2021)</a>, abandons $\ell_p$ altogether. It trains the model against attacks bounded by LPIPS distance. PAT is trained on perceptual attacks only, yet it generalizes to five types of attacks it did not train on: $\ell_\infty$, $\ell_2$, spatial attack <a class="cite" href="#ref-geometric-engstrom">Engstrom et al. (2019)</a>; <a class="cite" href="#ref-geometric-stadv">Xiao et al. (2018b)</a>, recoloring <a class="cite" href="#ref-color-first-one-cvprw-2018">Hosseini &amp; Poovendran (2018)</a>; <a class="cite" href="#ref-color-functional-attack-nips-2019">Laidlaw &amp; Feizi (2019)</a>; <a class="cite" href="#ref-color-semantic-manipulation-iclr-2020">Bhattad et al. (2020)</a>, and JPEG <a class="cite" href="#ref-elastic-attack">Kaufmann et al. (2019)</a>. It reached state-of-the-art robustness on their union back then. This is the broadest cross-family transfer shown by any defense in this group. The transfer holds because these attacks fall inside the perceptual (LPIPS) ball. It has not been tested against UAEs that lie outside that ball, such as geometric pose attacks or diffusion-based content edits. Even so, replacing the $\ell_p$ constraint with a perceptual one may be the more promising direction for defending against UAEs in general. It aligns the training constraint with human perception rather than pixel distance, which is closer to what unifies the UAE threat model. Liu et al. <a class="cite" href="#ref-physical-parameter-based-parametric-adversaries">Liu et al. (2019)</a> use parametric norm-balls over physical 3D-rendering parameters. They show that adversarial training in that space improves robustness to real-world physical variations. ManiFool <a class="cite" href="#ref-geometric-manifool">Kanbak et al. (2018)</a> establishes a comparable result for rigid geometric transformations. Adversarial training on worst-case rotations, translations, and scalings improves robustness to such transformations.

The mechanism underlying these defenses is, with very few exceptions, adversarial training. The main methodological variable is which UAE generator supplies the training-time adversaries. In most cases, the defense inherits robustness to the same family it trains on, whether on-manifold, style-transferred, or parametric physical perturbations. Some of these defenses also transfer beyond their training attack. DMAT and the Joint Space Attack improve robustness to unseen color, filter, and $\ell_p$-type attacks. PAT shows the widest transfer, reaching spatial, recoloring, and $\ell_p$ attacks from a single perceptual constraint. This transfer is real, but it stays within small or near-imperceptible perturbations. No defense in this group has been shown to transfer across the wider UAE space, for example to geometric pose attacks or diffusion-based content edits. The perceptual approach of PAT may point the more promising way forward. It replaces the $\ell_p$ constraint with one closer to human perception, which is nearer to what the UAE threat model has in common.

<h3 id="section-4-3">4.3 The Absence of a General-Purpose UAE Defense</h3>

The failure of $\ell_p$-calibrated defenses against UAEs is not a deployment issue that better hyperparameters can resolve. It is a mismatch between the geometry the defense formalizes and the geometry the attacker exploits. The defenses proposed specifically for UAEs inherit a version of the same problem. Most are calibrated to a particular UAE family, and that calibration transfers only partway. Some methods do generalize beyond the attack they train on. DMAT and the Joint Space Attack reach unseen color, filter, and $\ell_p$-type attacks, and PAT reaches spatial, recoloring, and $\ell_p$ attacks from a single perceptual constraint. This transfer is real, but it stays within small perturbations. UAEs span a much wider space. They include color manipulations, photometric and filter-based transformations, geometric and spatial warpings, distance-metric redefinitions, style and content edits, and generative synthesis in GAN, normalizing-flow, and diffusion latent spaces. No existing defense covers more than a slice of this space, and the slices barely overlap. Defending against UAEs in general is a genuinely open problem. This is not because no single defense has yet succeeded, but because the threat model itself is heterogeneous and resists the unified geometric framing that made $\ell_p$ defense tractable.

Among current approaches, perceptual adversarial training <a class="cite" href="#ref-distance-metric-lpips">Laidlaw et al. (2021)</a> may point the more promising way forward. By replacing the $\ell_p$ constraint with a perceptual one, it aligns the training constraint with human perception rather than pixel distance, which is closer to what the UAE threat model has in common. This difficulty also motivates a complementary line of inquiry. If UAEs reveal that classifiers are sensitive to high-level semantic, stylistic, and geometric features that no pixel-space defense can isolate, then a principled defense ultimately requires understanding how these features are represented and used inside the model. This connects directly to the mechanistic-interpretability thread that this survey takes up in subsequent sections. Characterizing the internal computations that respond to color, style, geometry, and semantic content is a prerequisite for any defense that aspires to generalize across the full breadth of the UAE space, rather than against one slice of it at a time.

<h2 id="section-5">5 Evaluation Metrics and Benchmarking</h2>

UAEs challenge the traditional evaluation paradigm of adversarial robustness. $\ell_p$-bounded attacks use small perturbations. UAEs instead use large perturbations that stay semantically meaningful. This requires an evaluation framework that goes beyond attack success rates. We propose a four criteria conceptual framework. It covers four things. The first is attack effectiveness, the ability to induce misclassification. The second is perceptual realism and semantic fidelity, meaning visual plausibility and semantic consistency. The third is perturbation flexibility, the degree of freedom under perceptual, structural, or latent-space constraints. The fourth is operational efficiency, the computational cost, and practical feasibility of attack generation. We group these four criteria into three reported dimensions, which give the framework its name <strong>FAIR</strong>: perturbation <strong>F</strong>lexibility; <strong>A</strong>ttack efficacy (measures attack success rate &amp; operational efficiency ); and <strong>I</strong>mage <strong>R</strong>ealism, which combines perceptual plausibility with semantic fidelity. We propose FAIR as a unified evaluation metric for future UAE research. It addresses the current lack of standardized evaluation protocols. Most prior studies do not assess all three dimensions. Perturbation flexibility, in particular, is often neglected.

<h3 id="section-5-1">5.1 Attack Effectiveness</h3>

<strong>Attack Success Rate (ASR)</strong> is the ratio of misclassified examples to total attempts. It is evaluated under white-box and black-box settings. White-box means full model access. Black-box means access to model outputs only, whether through queries or through a surrogate. <strong>Transferability</strong> measures how well adversarial examples crafted on a white-box surrogate model generalize to unseen black-box models. It is usually measured by ASR across black-box target models. <strong>Operational efficiency</strong> measures the computational and time costs of generating adversarial examples. UAEs often rely on generative models such as GANs or diffusion models. For these methods, efficiency is assessed by training time when retraining is required. For methods that use pretrained models, it is assessed by per-example generation time.

<h3 id="section-5-2">5.2 Perceptual Realism &amp; Semantic Fidelity</h3>

UAEs are visually realistic. This realism follows from the large perturbations they use. However, the same large perturbations can also introduce unnatural distortions. Evaluation must therefore measure how realistic an image looks. Standard $\ell_p$ distortion metrics cannot measure this, so evaluation borrows metrics from image generation research instead. These metrics usually combine quantitative measures with human judgment.

<h4 id="section-5-2-1">5.2.1 Quantitative Metrics</h4>

Quantitative realism metrics divide by whether they require the clean image as a reference. The FAIR framework request the image quality evaluation design to respect this division but not any single metric. The choice of metric should follow the specific perturbation type because different UAE variants may need tailored evaluation.

<strong>Full-reference metrics</strong> LPIPS <a class="cite" href="#ref-lpips">Zhang et al. (2018)</a>, SSIM <a class="cite" href="#ref-ssim">Wang et al. (2004)</a>, PSNR, and RMSE measure distance to a specific clean image and are therefore appropriate for reference-anchored UAE families where the clean image remains a meaningful anchor, notably distance metric-based attacks, which are defined by such a bound, and latent perturbations of a given input. These metrics are not appropriate where the perturbation alters the referenced content, as in geometric transformation, style transfer, and content-editing attacks, or where no reference exists, as in from-scratch synthesis.

<strong>No-reference metrics</strong> No-reference metrics such as NIQE <a class="cite" href="#ref-niqe">Mittal et al. (2013)</a>, BRISQUE <a class="cite" href="#ref-brisque">Mittal et al. (2012)</a>, PIQE <a class="cite" href="#ref-piqe">Venkatanath et al. (2015)</a>, and NIMA <a class="cite" href="#ref-iqa-nima">Talebi &amp; Milanfar (2018)</a>, along with distributional metrics such as FID <a class="cite" href="#ref-fid">Heusel et al. (2017)</a> and KID <a class="cite" href="#ref-kid">Bińkowski et al. (2018)</a>, evaluate image quality without requiring a reference and are therefore the appropriate metrics for UAEs that alter content or are generated from scratch.

<strong>Semantic alignment metrics</strong> Generative-model-based UAEs, especially those generated from scratch, require a check for semantic validity, as they may suffer from class-swap failures in which a perturbation preserves natural appearance but changes the semantic class (e.g., an image with the ground-truth label of a fish perturbed to resemble a palm tree). VQA Score <a class="cite" href="#ref-vqascore">Lin et al. (2024b)</a> and CLIP Score <a class="cite" href="#ref-clipscore">Hessel et al. (2021)</a> address this problem and should accompany realism metrics whenever the attack has the freedom to change content, particularly for from-scratch generative UAEs.

We introduce representative metrics from each group as a reference below.

<strong>FID</strong> <a class="cite" href="#ref-fid">Heusel et al. (2017)</a> measures image quality. It compares the feature distributions of real and generated images. It uses Inception-v3 features, fits a multivariate Gaussian to each set of features, and computes the Fréchet distance between the two Gaussians, which, for Gaussians, coincides with the 2-Wasserstein distance. A lower score indicates higher quality and greater diversity.

<strong>LPIPS</strong> <a class="cite" href="#ref-lpips">Zhang et al. (2018)</a> measures perceptual similarity. It compares deep feature representations rather than pixel-level or structural differences. The score is a weighted distance between the feature maps of two images. Lower values indicate greater perceptual similarity. This makes LPIPS well-suited for evaluating UAEs, where perturbations may be large yet visually plausible.

<strong>VQA Score</strong> <a class="cite" href="#ref-vqascore">Lin et al. (2024b)</a> and <strong>CLIP Score</strong> <a class="cite" href="#ref-clipscore">Hessel et al. (2021)</a> evaluate semantic alignment between images and text. This makes them effective for detecting class-swap failures. VQA Score uses a visual question answering model to probe semantic content through natural language queries. CLIP Score computes the cosine similarity between image and text embeddings from the CLIP model <a class="cite" href="#ref-clip-model">Radford et al. (2021)</a>. A high score indicates that the adversarial example preserves the semantic content of the original label. These evaluation metrics are best suited for UAEs generated without a reference image through generative models. In that setting the class-swap problem often occurs.

We list these families rather than a single default pair, because the appropriate metric depends on whether the attack preserves a reference. The best metric depends on the specific method and need. The Image Quality Assessment (IQA) toolbox <a class="cite" href="#ref-iqa-package-github">Chen &amp; Mo (2022)</a> provides many IQA methods for evaluation, both with and without reference images. Please refer to it to find the metric that best fits a given UAE method.

<h4 id="section-5-2-2">5.2.2 Human Evaluation</h4>

Human evaluation remains the gold standard for image quality assessment. It is usually done through user studies. In these studies, participants distinguish real from adversarial images or compare outputs across methods. Such studies are costly, and few existing UAE works use them. Building efficient human-centered evaluation frameworks remains an open problem.

<h3 id="section-5-3">5.3 Perturbation Flexibility</h3>

<figure id="figure-11"><a class="figure-link" href="/blog/beyond-the-lp-ball/figure-11.webp"><img src="/blog/beyond-the-lp-ball/figure-11.webp" alt="Tree diagram grouping UAE methods into four levels of perturbation flexibility, from explicit distance constraints to latent manifold optimization." width="2000" height="2370" loading="lazy" decoding="async"></a><figcaption><strong>Figure 11:</strong> Taxonomy of perturbation flexibility of UAEs. Methods are grouped by the level of flexibility their constraint admits. <span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><semantics><mrow><mo>†</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\dagger</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="katex-base"><span class="katex-strut" style="height:0.8889em;vertical-align:-0.1944em;"></span><span class="mord">†</span></span></span></span> marks methods that do not strictly meet the UAE criterion of Section <a href="#section-2">2</a> and are surveyed for methodological continuity. Read against Figure <a href="#figure-5">5</a>, this figure shows that the perturbation flexibility of a method does not always strictly align with its generation mechanism group: the style transfer and content editing family and the generative-model-based methods spans three levels(Level I, III and IV).</figcaption></figure>

Although UAEs are not confined to an $\ell_p$ norm ball in pixel space, they are still subject to constraints that keep them plausible. We introduce a taxonomy, shown in Figure <a href="#figure-11">11</a>, that groups UAEs by how flexible their perturbations are. Perturbation flexibility reflects how constrained the search space is. It is separate from the magnitude of the resulting pixel changes. A method with high flexibility can still produce small pixel changes, and a method with low flexibility can still produce large ones. We rank the four categories below from the strictest constraint to the loosest.

1. <strong>I. Explicit Distance Constraints (Low flexibility):</strong><br>The search space is limited by a small, explicitly bounded neighborhood of the clean image $x$: the method enforces $d(x, x')\le \epsilon$ for a fixed distance $d$. Most UAE methods at this level replace the $\ell_p$ norm in pixel space with a metric that correlates better with human vision, such as Wasserstein distance <a class="cite" href="#ref-distance-metric-wassestein">Wong et al. (2019)</a>, CIEDE2000 <a class="cite" href="#ref-distance-metric-percl">Zhao et al. (2020a)</a>, or LPIPS <a class="cite" href="#ref-distance-metric-lpips">Laidlaw et al. (2021)</a>. There are more methods at this level. Methods that keep an explicit $\ell_p$ budget and use a UAE mechanism only as a realism regularizer have the same limitation as a conventional attack. They are all grouped in the leaf node of “Retained $\ell_p$ budget”, under “Pixel space” category in Figure <a href="#figure-11">11</a>. A fixed, deterministic transformation with no free parameters, such as pixel inversion <a class="cite" href="#ref-color-negative-images">Hosseini et al. (2017)</a>, has the tightest perturbation space. The perturbation flexibility measures the size of the admissible search space, not the size of the resulting pixel change.
2. <strong>II. Parametric Space Constraints (Moderate flexibility):</strong><br>These attacks restrict perturbations to a small set of explicit parameters ($k \ll d$). The adversary does not search the high-dimensional RGB pixel space ($\mathbb{R}^d$). Instead it works over a smaller space defined by physical, geometric, non-RGB color, or functional attributes.
