Examples include rotation angles $\theta$, geometric coordinates $V$, physical or scene parameters, or filter coefficients. This space is usually modeled as a differentiable function $G(x, a)$. Here $x$ is the original input image, and $a \in \mathbb{R}^k$ is the small set of parameters the adversary optimizes over. The function $G$ applies the parameters $a$ to the image $x$ to produce the adversarial image. Optimization proceeds by gradient ascent. When the parameter set is non-differentiable, grid search is used instead. The resulting perturbations can produce large pixel-space changes, such as color, lighting, geometric, contrast, or scene transformations. Such changes are generally perceptible but remain semantically meaningful.
3. <strong>III. Semantic Constraints (Explicit) (High flexibility):</strong><br>These attacks limit the perturbation space so that changes keep high-level image semantics. The constraints separate pixel-level freedom from perceptual coherence. Perturbations are unbounded in $\ell_p$ terms but remain plausible because the attack enforces structural or semantic uniformity. The constraints target specific attributes, such as facial features or image texture. This allows large but coherent perturbations.
4. <strong>IV. Latent Manifold Optimization (Implicit) (Very high flexibility):</strong><br>These attacks work inside the representation manifold defined by advanced generative models (e.g., GANs, diffusion models). Rather than perturbing pixels directly, the adversary optimizes the latent code ($z$) or intermediate features. This forces $x'$ to lie on the learned data manifold $\mathcal{M}$. Because $\mathcal{M}$ captures complex semantic structure, the image stays photorealistic even when the pixel-space perturbation is large. The perturbation magnitude can therefore be unrestricted. Some methods add explicit constraints as well, such as an $\ell_p$ norm in latent space. The cost of this flexibility is a large increase in computation.

The four categories form a single ordering from the strictest constraint to the loosest. This ordering gives FAIR a concrete axis for perturbation flexibility. Most prior UAE work reports only attack success and image quality, so this axis is often left unmeasured.

<h3 id="section-5-4">5.4 Benchmark Datasets</h3>

We describe the datasets that recur most often across the surveyed methods. Several methods additionally use domain-specific sets: Places365 <a class="cite" href="#ref-dataset-places365">Zhou et al. (2018)</a>, LSUN <a class="cite" href="#ref-dataset-lsun">Yu et al. (2015)</a>, PubFig <a class="cite" href="#ref-dataset-pubfig">Kumar et al. (2009)</a>, AFHQ <a class="cite" href="#ref-dataset-afhq">Choi et al. (2020)</a>, and medical imaging collections, which we note in Tables 3–5 but do not describe here. There are seven most used datasets in the surveyed papers. Five are standard image classification datasets: MNIST <a class="cite" href="#ref-dataset-mnist">LeCun et al. (1998)</a>, CIFAR-10, CIFAR-100 <a class="cite" href="#ref-dataset-cifar">Krizhevsky et al. (2009)</a>, ImageNet <a class="cite" href="#ref-dataset-imagenet">Deng et al. (2009)</a>, and ImageNet-Compatible<sup class="fn"><a href="#footnote-2" id="footnote-ref-2" aria-label="Footnote 2">2</a></sup>. Two are widely used in face recognition: CelebA <a class="cite" href="#ref-dataset-celebA">Liu et al. (2015)</a> and CelebA-HQ <a class="cite" href="#ref-dataset-celebA-HQ">Karras et al. (2018)</a>. Table <a href="#table-2">2</a> lists their class counts, image counts, resolutions, and release years. The descriptions below cover only the distinctive properties of each dataset that the table does not capture.

<strong>MNIST</strong> <a class="cite" href="#ref-dataset-mnist">LeCun et al. (1998)</a> is a dataset of handwritten digits, one class per digit from 0 to 9. The images are grayscale. The digits are size-normalized and centered in a fixed-size frame. MNIST is small and easy to classify, so it serves as an early testbed for attack and defense methods.

<strong>CIFAR-10</strong> <a class="cite" href="#ref-dataset-cifar">Krizhevsky et al. (2009)</a> is a dataset of natural color images spanning everyday object categories, such as animals and vehicles. It is a labeled subset of the larger Tiny Images collection <a class="cite" href="#ref-dataset-tiny-image">Torralba et al. (2008)</a>. The images are more varied than MNIST but remain low-resolution. The related CIFAR-100 dataset shares the same images and format but splits them into 100 finer classes grouped under 20 superclasses.

<strong>ImageNet</strong> <a class="cite" href="#ref-dataset-imagenet">Deng et al. (2009)</a>, in its ILSVRC subset, is a large-scale recognition dataset of natural photographs. The 1,000 classes are drawn from the WordNet hierarchy and do not overlap. The images vary in size and are usually resized before use. ImageNet is large and high-resolution, so it is the main benchmark for evaluating UAEs at scale.

<strong>ImageNet-Compatible</strong> is a small evaluation set introduced for the NIPS 2017 adversarial competition. Its images follow the ImageNet label format and share its 1,000 classes. The set is designed for adversarial evaluation rather than training. Its small size makes it a convenient benchmark for testing attacks against ImageNet-trained models. The evaluation set used in the UAE literature is the 1,000 image DEV split.

<strong>CelebA</strong> <a class="cite" href="#ref-dataset-celebA">Liu et al. (2015)</a> is a large-scale face attribute dataset built from celebrity photographs. Each image carries 40 binary attribute labels and 5 landmark locations. The images cover a wide range of poses and background clutter. CelebA supports face recognition, attribute editing, and face synthesis, so it is often used to evaluate UAEs on face-based tasks.

<strong>CelebA-HQ</strong> <a class="cite" href="#ref-dataset-celebA-HQ">Karras et al. (2018)</a> is a high-quality version of CelebA. Its images were selected from CelebA and refined to a much higher resolution through progressive GAN processing. It retains CelebA’s attribute labels for the images it selects, though it covers a subset of CelebA’s identities. The high resolution makes CelebA-HQ suitable for evaluating UAEs that rely on high-fidelity generative models.

<h2 id="section-6">6 Performance Evaluation and Comparison</h2>

<h3 id="section-6-1">6.1 Evaluation Practices in the Surveyed Methods Under the FAIR Framework</h3>

The tables below record what each attack method in Section <a href="#section-3">3</a> evaluates, and how. We organize the record by the FAIR dimensions. The first is perturbation <strong>F</strong>lexibility, reported as Level I–IV. We assign this dimension ourselves, based on our definition of perturbation flexibility in Section <a href="#section-5-3">5.3</a>, because the source papers do not evaluate it. The second dimension is <strong>A</strong>ttack efficacy. It covers whether a paper reports ASR, transferability, and efficiency or computation cost. We do not report the exact ASR numbers. Each paper uses a different dataset, target model, and protocol. The ASR numbers in the original papers are therefore not comparable across methods. The third dimension is <strong>I</strong>mage <strong>R</strong>ealism. It covers whether a method evaluates image quality through quantitative metrics, human studies, or both. Each column marks whether a paper evaluates a given dimension. Methods that do not strictly meet the UAE definition, or that are defense-oriented, are marked in the Notes column.

<figure class="table-figure wide" id="table-4"><figcaption><strong>Table 4:</strong> FAIR evaluation-practice summary (2/3): style transfer and content editing-based UAEs. All columns record whether a paper evaluates a dimension, not how well it scores. Numbers are omitted because protocols are not comparable across papers. ASR rep. = attack success rate reported; Tr. = transferability evaluated; Eff. = efficiency/cost reported; Human = human study conducted. For defense-oriented methods, we record the data and models they use to evaluate robustness. ASR does not apply to them and is marked (–). Threat model abbreviations: W and B refer to white-box and black-box attacks, respectively, W<span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><semantics><mrow><mo>→</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\to</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="katex-base"><span class="katex-strut" style="height:0.3669em;"></span><span class="mrel">→</span></span></span></span>B denotes a transfer-based attack crafted white-box on a surrogate and evaluated black-box on the target. T and U refer to targeted and untargeted attacks, respectively. Dataset abbreviations: toy refers to MNIST, CIFAR-10, and CIFAR-100. IN = ImageNet; IN-C = ImageNet-Compatible; IN-100 = the 100-class subset of IN; CA = CelebA; CA-HQ = CelebA-HQ; LSUN, PubFig, AFHQ as named by the original papers; “domain(<span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><semantics><mrow><mo>⋅</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\cdot</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="katex-base"><span class="katex-strut" style="height:0.4445em;"></span><span class="mord">⋅</span></span></span></span>)” denotes a domain specific dataset. We report only the largest dataset used by each method. For example, if a method is evaluated on both a toy dataset and ImageNet, we report only IN.</figcaption><div class="table-scroll" tabindex="0" role="region" aria-label="Table 4"><table><thead><tr><th>Method</th><th>Subcategory</th><th>Venue/Year</th><th>Threat</th><th class="c">Flex.</th><th>Datasets</th><th>Target models</th><th>ASR rep.</th><th class="c">Tr.</th><th class="c">Eff.</th><th>Realism metrics</th><th class="c">Human</th><th class="c">Code</th><th>Notes</th></tr></thead><tbody><tr><td>tAdv <a class="cite" href="#ref-color-semantic-manipulation-iclr-2020">Bhattad et al. <figure class="table-figure wide" id="table-5"><figcaption><strong>Table 5:</strong> FAIR evaluation-practice summary (3/3): generative model-based UAEs and historically related perturbation generators. All columns record whether a paper evaluates a dimension, not how well it scores. Numbers are omitted because protocols are not comparable across papers. ASR rep. = attack success rate reported; Tr. = transferability evaluated; Eff. = efficiency/cost reported; Human = human study conducted. Threat model abbreviations: W and B refer to white-box and black-box attacks, respectively. T and U refer to targeted and untargeted attacks, respectively. Dataset abbreviations: toy refers to MNIST, CIFAR-10, and CIFAR-100. IN = ImageNet; IN-C = ImageNet-Compatible; IN-100 = the 100-class subset of IN; CA = CelebA; CA-HQ = CelebA-HQ; LSUN, PubFig, AFHQ as named by the original papers; “domain(<span class="katex"><span class="katex-mathml"><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><semantics><mrow><mo>⋅</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\cdot</annotation></semantics></math></span><span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="katex-base"><span class="katex-strut" style="height:0.4445em;"></span><span class="mord">⋅</span></span></span></span>)” denotes a domain specific dataset. We report only the largest dataset used by each method. For example, if a method is evaluated on both a toy dataset and ImageNet, we report only IN.</figcaption><div class="table-scroll" tabindex="0" role="region" aria-label="Table 5"><table><thead><tr><th>Method</th><th>Subcategory</th><th>Venue/Year</th><th>Threat</th><th class="c">Flex.</th><th>Datasets</th><th>Target models</th><th>ASR rep.</th><th class="c">Tr.</th><th class="c">Eff.</th><th>Realism metrics</th><th class="c">Human</th><th class="c">Code</th><th>Notes</th></tr></thead><tbody><tr><td>Song et al. <a class="cite" href="#ref-uae_origin">Song et al. <h3 id="section-6-2">6.2 Discussion: Trends and Gaps in Evaluation Practice</h3>

This evaluation does not rank the surveyed methods. The tables above record whether each method reports a dimension, not how well it scores on it. We discuss the three FAIR dimensions in turn, and then the trends that cut across all three tables. Two findings organize the discussion. First, perturbation flexibility is determined by the constraint a method imposes on its perturbation space, not by the taxonomy category it belongs to. These two axes are aligned in most cases, though there are a few exceptions. Second, the most recent UAE methods tend to cover more evaluation dimensions, and image realism is the clearest case. Realism reporting begins in earnest around 2021. Of the methods published before that year, only ReColorAdv <a class="cite" href="#ref-color-functional-attack-nips-2019">Laidlaw &amp; Feizi (2019)</a> and ColorFool <a class="cite" href="#ref-color-colorfool-cvpr-2020">Shamsabadi et al. (2020b)</a> report a quantitative realism metric; from 2021 onward the majority do.

<strong>Perturbation flexibility.</strong> The taxonomy introduced in Section <a href="#section-3">3</a> groups methods by how a perturbation is generated. In Section <a href="#section-5-3">5.3</a>, the levels of perturbation flexibility are defined by how much freedom the perturbation is permitted. These two axes correlate, but they do not coincide. Each divergence between them is informative. Only one category maps onto a single level by construction. Every distance metric-based UAE method in Table <a href="#table-3">3</a> sits at Level I, because the category is defined by its constraint. Wasserstein Attack <a class="cite" href="#ref-distance-metric-wassestein">Wong et al. (2019)</a>, PerC-AL <a class="cite" href="#ref-distance-metric-percl">Zhao et al. (2020a)</a>, NPTM <a class="cite" href="#ref-distance-metric-lpips">Laidlaw et al. (2021)</a>, and Demiguise <a class="cite" href="#ref-distance-metric-demiguise">Wang et al. (2021)</a> differ in the metric they bound, but all replace the $\ell_p$ ball in pixel space with an $\ell_p$ ball in another space. So their generation technique and their constraint are the same object. Other types of UAE methods do not follow this. UAE methods in the same category may instead have different levels of perturbation flexibility. Most transformation-based and physical parameter-based attacks occupy Level II, since they search a small explicit parameter space of colors, filter coefficients, rigid transforms, or rendering parameters. Yet Negative images <a class="cite" href="#ref-color-negative-images">Hosseini et al. (2017)</a> falls to Level I within the same category. The reason is that a fixed, deterministic inversion has no parameters to search at all. Its perturbation is large in pixel space. Its flexibility is essentially zero. Flexibility is the freedom the adversary has in choosing the perturbation. This case makes the general point concrete. Flexibility measures the size of the admissible search space. It does not measure the size of the resulting pixel change.

The style transfer and content editing family in Table <a href="#table-4">4</a> shows the widest spread, spanning Level I to Level IV within a single category. When the style operation is confined to a fixed parametric space or a bounded feature-space budget, as in tAdv <a class="cite" href="#ref-color-semantic-manipulation-iclr-2020">Bhattad et al. (2020)</a> and FSA <a class="cite" href="#ref-style-perturbation-feature-space">Xu et al. (2021)</a>, flexibility stays at Level II or III. When the method retains an explicit $\ell_p$ bound and uses stylization only to improve transferability or training, as StyLess <a class="cite" href="#ref-style-augmentation-styless">Liang &amp; Xiao (2023)</a>, SAT <a class="cite" href="#ref-style-augmentation-SAT">Naseer et al. (2022)</a>, an STM <a class="cite" href="#ref-style-augmentation-STM">Ge et al. (2023)</a>, it drops to Level I, as the Notes column records, out of the strict UAE definition altogether. When the style or content edit is optimized in a generative latent space, as CLIP2Protect <a class="cite" href="#ref-content-ediging-clip2protect">Shamshad et al. (2023)</a>, ACA <a class="cite" href="#ref-content-editding-ACA">Chen et al. (2023b)</a>, and I2A <a class="cite" href="#ref-content-editing-instruct2attack">Liu et al. (2023b)</a>, it reaches Level IV. The generative family in Table <a href="#table-5">5</a> shows a similar version of this pattern. Nearly every UAE belongs to Level IV. However, DiffPGD <a class="cite" href="#ref-diffpgd">Xue et al. (2023)</a> is in Level I despite a diffusion backbone. It keeps an $\ell_p$ budget in pixel space. It uses the diffusion process inside PGD as a realism regularizer rather than as a search space. These examples show that using generative models to produce UAEs does not automatically grant more perturbation flexibility. What sets the flexibility level is the constraint the method chooses to impose. The broad trend is that flexibility rises with the sophistication of the transformation.

<strong>Attack efficacy.</strong> Every surveyed attack reports an attack success rate. This makes ASR the least discriminating entry in the record. The remaining two efficacy signals are reported far less consistently. Their distribution is again structured. Transferability is evaluated by about two-thirds of the attacks. Recent work evaluates it more often, as black-box threat models have become the expected baseline. Within Table <a href="#table-3">3</a>, we can see that nearly every color, filter, and lighting attack evaluates the transferability, while most geometric transformation-based attacks omit it. The asymmetry is consistent with how the two subcategories operate. Photometric shifts perturb distributional statistics that different models plausibly share, so transfer is a natural claim to test, whereas a spatial flow field is optimized against one model’s specific spatial sensitivity, and authors appear not to expect it to transfer. The tables record evaluation practice rather than outcomes. The practice encodes the field’s implicit beliefs about which perturbations generalize across models. Efficiency is reported by only a minority of methods. That minority is concentrated in the generative model-based methods of Table <a href="#table-5">5</a>. The sampling cost is the most evident liability in this type of UAEs, and authors have a clear reason to measure it. Transformation-based and style-based attacks seldom report cost at all. This is presumably because parametric search is assumed to be cheap. However, UAEs are frequently expensive to produce. Yet the cost of an attack is almost never placed next to its success rate. A reader, therefore, cannot tell whether a high ASR was obtained cheaply or through heavy per-image optimization.

<strong>Image realism.</strong> Image realism is the least standardized of the three dimensions. No single metric is shared across the field. The measures in use include LPIPS <a class="cite" href="#ref-lpips">Zhang et al. (2018)</a>, SSIM <a class="cite" href="#ref-ssim">Wang et al. (2004)</a>, PSNR, RMSE, FID <a class="cite" href="#ref-fid">Heusel et al. (2017)</a>, KID <a class="cite" href="#ref-kid">Bińkowski et al. (2018)</a>, NIQE <a class="cite" href="#ref-niqe">Mittal et al. (2013)</a>, BRISQUE <a class="cite" href="#ref-brisque">Mittal et al. (2012)</a>, PIQE <a class="cite" href="#ref-piqe">Venkatanath et al. (2015)</a>, NIMA <a class="cite" href="#ref-iqa-nima">Talebi &amp; Milanfar (2018)</a>, MUSIQ <a class="cite" href="#ref-musiq">Ke et al. (2021)</a>, HyperIQA <a class="cite" href="#ref-hyperiqa">Su et al. (2020)</a>, DISTS <a class="cite" href="#ref-iqa-dists">Ding et al. (2020)</a>, TReS <a class="cite" href="#ref-tres">Golestaneh et al. (2022)</a>. A paper typically reports one or two of these, and rarely the same pair twice. Fewer than half of the transformation-based attacks in Table <a href="#table-3">3</a> report any quantitative realism metric. The rest rely on a small number of qualitative figures. A deeper concern underlies this inconsistency. Several of the most widely used metrics are full-reference. LPIPS, SSIM, PSNR, and RMSE all measure distance to a clean reference image. For an unrestricted attack the reference is exactly what changes. These metrics therefore measure the wrong quantity. They penalize legitimate semantic edits as though they were distortion. No-reference metrics such as NIQE, BRISQUE, PIQE, and NIMA fit the setting better, yet they are used less often. Human studies remain the only direct measure of perceptual realism. They appear in fewer than a third of the surveyed methods. They are most likely omitted because human evaluation is costly.

<strong>Toward a standardized evaluation protocol.</strong> The audit exposes a structural problem in the evaluation of UAEs. The field has no unified evaluation protocol for them, and so no comparable basis for assessing their performance. A method may report a high ASR and strong image realism while omitting generation cost. A reader then cannot tell whether those results came cheaply or at the expense of substantial per-image optimization. This is the very trade-off that Attack efficacy is meant to capture, and it stays invisible whenever a protocol leaves operation efficiency unmeasured. Section <a href="#section-7-2">7.2</a> takes up what it would require to close this.

<h2 id="section-7">7 Open Challenges and Future Directions</h2>

Surveying UAE methods reveals several unresolved challenges in this field. This section examines the four most pressing. They fall into two groups. The first concerns coverage: no defense spans the full breadth of the threat model (Section <a href="#section-7-1">7.1</a>), and no attack pipeline spans more than one generator’s prior (Section <a href="#section-7-4">7.4</a>). The second concerns measurement: results are reported under protocols that do not compose (Section <a href="#section-7-2">7.2</a>), and the human judgment on which the UAE definition rests is not standardized (Section <a href="#section-7-3">7.3</a>). For each challenge, we not only state the problem but also propose a concrete first step toward resolving it. Where a challenge admits no clear solution, we leave it open. The proposed steps double as directions for future research.

<h3 id="section-7-1">7.1 Toward a Broad-Coverage Defense</h3>

Every defense that has generalized beyond the attack it was trained on works in a similar way. Instead of bounding the perturbation in pixel space, it bounds the perturbation in some other space, and it becomes robust to what that space covers. Section <a href="#section-4">4</a> documented three such defenses. Perceptual adversarial training <a class="cite" href="#ref-distance-metric-lpips">Laidlaw et al. (2021)</a> replaces the $\ell_p$ bound with an LPIPS bound, and the resulting model resists spatial, recoloring, and $\ell_p$ attacks that it never trained against. Dual-manifold <a class="cite" href="#ref-genmodel-reference-dual">Lin et al. (2020)</a> and joint-space <a class="cite" href="#ref-genmodel-reference-IJSAT">Lau et al. (2023)</a> training use a latent-space bound instead, and resist unseen color, filter, and $\ell_p$ attacks. Generalizing across attack families is therefore possible. However, the generalization is limited: in the reported case, the unseen types of attacks still stay close to the source image under the defense’s own metric, and none of these cases involves a geometric pose change, a style transfer, or a diffusion-based content edit.

Certification-based defense that generalizes to non-$\ell_p$-bounded attacks works in the same way. Section <a href="#section-4-1">4.1</a> describes three methods that certify over parameterized transformation families <a class="cite" href="#ref-certified-semantic-deepg">Balunovic et al. (2019)</a>; <a class="cite" href="#ref-certified-semantic-semantifynn">Mohapatra et al. (2020)</a>; <a class="cite" href="#ref-certified-semantic-tss">Li et al. (2021)</a>. All of these transformations belong to the photometric and geometric subcategories of our taxonomy (Sections <a href="#section-3-1-2">3.1.2</a> and <a href="#section-3-1-4">3.1.4</a>). Each of these methods also requires the transformation family to be written analytically and to have few parameters, which does not apply to the style, content-editing, and generative categories. Both lines of work therefore cover only what their constraint space covers. A defense with UAE-scale coverage would need a constraint space at least as expressive as all the generators used to attack it taken together. Section <a href="#section-8">8</a> develops one direction that follows from this: if UAEs succeed by exploiting features that classifiers rely on, then a defense would have to operate on those features rather than on the pixel statistics surrounding them.

<h3 id="section-7-2">7.2 The Missing Benchmark</h3>

UAE research has no standardized benchmark for the generated attack. Building one is hard because no single score applies across categories. For example, in terms of perceptual realism, the categories of Section <a href="#section-3">3</a> cannot be compared along one perceptual axis. As Section <a href="#section-5">5</a> sets out, reference-based metrics such as LPIPS <a class="cite" href="#ref-lpips">Zhang et al. (2018)</a> and SSIM <a class="cite" href="#ref-ssim">Wang et al. (2004)</a> penalize the semantic and geometric edits that define several UAE families. FID <a class="cite" href="#ref-fid">Heusel et al. (2017)</a> and NIQE <a class="cite" href="#ref-niqe">Mittal et al. (2013)</a> do not measure whether an individual sample keeps its class. VQAScore <a class="cite" href="#ref-vqascore">Lin et al. (2024b)</a> and CLIP Score <a class="cite" href="#ref-clipscore">Hessel et al. (2021)</a> are proposed there to address class preservation. Both are computed from a vision-language model and inherit its biases. Both are computed from a vision-language model and inherit its biases, and neither has been validated against human judgment on adversarially edited images. Therefore, the dependence on a learned reference model that constrains LPIPS applies to them as well. No metric is therefore ready to serve across all categories. FAIR responds to this gap by fixing the axes such a benchmark reports. These are perturbation <strong>F</strong>lexibility, <strong>A</strong>ttack efficacy, which covers attack success together with generation cost, and <strong>I</strong>mage <strong>R</strong>ealism. But FAIR is a blueprint rather than an implementation. What it does not supply is the engineering. That means a curated dataset, a category-matched realism metric, released as a runnable protocol.

<h3 id="section-7-3">7.3 The Human Oracle</h3>

The UAE definition rests on human judgment. A perturbation qualifies only if a human still assigns the original label and still finds the image plausible. Human evaluation is therefore constitutive rather than supplementary. But it is not standardized. Of the 54 attack methods in Tables <a href="#table-3">3</a>–<a href="#table-5">5</a> that meet our UAE criterion, 16 report a human study. Our audit records whether one was run but not how, because there is no standard design to record against. Papers differ in annotator pool and count. They differ in whether participants supply a label or a naturalness rating. They differ in response format. A study measuring label preservation supports no claim about plausibility. A first step is a shared protocol with two tasks. In the first task, an annotator is shown an edited image and must choose a label for it. This checks label preservation. In the second task, an annotator is shown an edited image and an original image and must tell which one was edited. This checks plausibility. The two tasks map directly onto the UAE definition’s two conditions.

<h3 id="section-7-4">7.4 Coupling to Generative Backbones</h3>

An increasing share of UAE methods build the attack by searching the prior of one generative model. The diffusion-based methods (DiffAttack <a class="cite" href="#ref-diffattack">Chen et al. (2024)</a>, AdvDiff <a class="cite" href="#ref-advdiff">Dai et al. (2024)</a>, AdvDiffuser <a class="cite" href="#ref-advdiffuser">Chen et al. (2023a)</a>, ACA <a class="cite" href="#ref-content-editding-ACA">Chen et al. (2023b)</a>, Instruct2Attack <a class="cite" href="#ref-content-editing-instruct2attack">Liu et al. (2023b)</a>, SD-NAE <a class="cite" href="#ref-sd-nae">Lin et al. (2024a)</a>, VENOM <a class="cite" href="#ref-venom">Kuurila-Zhang et al. (2025)</a>) are typically built against a specific public Stable Diffusion checkpoint. The GAN-based ones are spread across backbones from AC-GAN <a class="cite" href="#ref-acgan">Odena et al. (2017)</a> for the earliest work, VAE-GAN <a class="cite" href="#ref-VAE-GAN">Larsen et al. (2016)</a> for on-manifold training, to StyleGAN <a class="cite" href="#ref-styleGAN">Karras et al. (2019)</a> for the face-editing attacks, and the normalizing-flow ones on Glow <a class="cite" href="#ref-glow">Kingma &amp; Dhariwal (2018)</a>. Each pipeline is tied to one backbone, and this tie has two costs. The first cost is limited scope. The attack is drawn from one generator’s prior, so it can only reach images that generator can produce. Its results therefore describe the perturbations reachable through that generator, not unrestricted perturbations in general. The pipeline is also fragile. When its generator is superseded, the attack becomes hard to reproduce or stops running altogether. The second cost concerns comparison across pipelines. Two attacks built on different generators report different numbers. Part of that difference comes from the generators. Part comes from the attacks. The current literature cannot tell us how much is which. No paper in our audit varies generator version, sampler, or guidance setting while holding the attack fixed. The protocol incompatibilities documented in Section <a href="#section-6">6</a> leave this unrecoverable after the fact. So when one method reports a higher success rate or better perceptual realism than another, we cannot say whether the attack is stronger or the generator is simply more capable. One way to separate the two is to report every attack across a shared panel of generators from different families and generations. This is hard in practice. An attack algorithm is often tightly coupled to its generator. It cannot be moved onto another generator unchanged. The problem therefore remains open.

<h2 id="section-8">8 Unrestricted Adversarial Examples as Diagnostic Tools for Mechanistic Interpretability</h2>

Mechanistic interpretability (MI) seeks to reverse-engineer neural networks by identifying the <em>features</em> (the concepts or input attributes represented by directions in the network’s activation space), the <em>circuits</em> (subgraphs of neurons, attention heads, and the weights between them that together implement a specific computation over those features), and ultimately the algorithms encoded in their weights <a class="cite" href="#ref-mi-circuits-olah2020">Olah et al. (2020)</a>; <a class="cite" href="#ref-mi-transformer-circuits2021">Elhage et al. (2021)</a>; <a class="cite" href="#ref-mi-automated-circuit-discovery2023">Conmy et al. (2023)</a>; <a class="cite" href="#ref-mi-bereska2024">Bereska &amp; Gavves (2024)</a>. MI has produced striking results for large language models, from circuit-level accounts of specific behaviors to interpretable features extracted at frontier scale <a class="cite" href="#ref-mi-ioi-circuit2022">Wang et al. (2023b)</a>; <a class="cite" href="#ref-mi-templeton2024">Templeton et al. (2024)</a>. Although the circuits program began in vision <a class="cite" href="#ref-mi-circuits-olah2020">Olah et al. (2020)</a>; <a class="cite" href="#ref-mi-circuits-thread2020">Cammarata et al. (2020)</a>, the modern toolchain has matured largely on language models, and its application to vision remains comparatively underexplored <a class="cite" href="#ref-mi-iclr-blogpost2025">Liu et al. (2025)</a>. This section offers a perspective on how UAE research could contribute to closing that gap. The discussion proceeds in three steps, summarized in Figure <a href="#figure-12">12</a>. Section <a href="#section-8-1">8.1</a> explains why MI needs structured visual interventions and why UAEs qualify as such interventions. Section <a href="#section-8-2">8.2</a> reviews what existing work has already established about the connection between adversarial examples and internal representations. Section <a href="#section-8-3">8.3</a> sketches how the taxonomy of Section <a href="#section-3">3</a> could serve as a source of candidate probes for vision MI: inputs crafted to reveal specific aspects of what a model computes internally.

<figure id="figure-12"><a class="figure-link" href="/blog/beyond-the-lp-ball/figure-12.webp"><img src="/blog/beyond-the-lp-ball/figure-12.webp" alt="Three-panel overview of the mechanistic interpretability section: established tools, demonstrated links between adversarial examples and representation geometry, and the outlook of UAE categories as probes." width="2000" height="1382" loading="lazy" decoding="async"></a><figcaption><strong>Figure 12:</strong> Overview of this section. <strong>Left (established):</strong> mechanistic interpretability supplies the concepts and causal tools (Section <a href="#section-8-1">8.1</a>). <strong>Middle (demonstrated):</strong> applying these tools to adversarial examples has established a link between representation geometry and attacks (Section <a href="#section-8-2">8.2</a>). <strong>Right (outlook):</strong> the UAE taxonomy of Section <a href="#section-3">3</a> could systematize such structured inputs into a three-step probe workflow (Section <a href="#section-8-3">8.3</a>). Solid boxes summarize established results; dashed boxes mark unresolved issues: the missing visual interventions, an empirical complication, and the obstacles the outlook must confront.</figcaption></figure>

<h3 id="section-8-1">8.1 From the Norm-bounded Perturbations to Structured Visual Interventions</h3>

A central challenge in MI is designing <em>interventions</em>. These are controlled manipulations that reveal how specific components contribute to model behavior. Two tools dominate such analysis. Activation patching copies internal activations from one input’s forward pass into another’s. This tests which components drive a behavior <a class="cite" href="#ref-mi-vig2020">Vig et al. (2020)</a>; <a class="cite" href="#ref-mi-rome2022">Meng et al. (2022)</a>; <a class="cite" href="#ref-mi-activation-patching2024">Heimersheim &amp; Nanda (2024)</a>; <a class="cite" href="#ref-mi-zhang-nanda2024">Zhang &amp; Nanda (2024)</a>. Sparse autoencoders (SAEs) are auxiliary networks. They break a model’s internal activations into a larger set of sparsely active, more interpretable components <a class="cite" href="#ref-mi-cunningham2024">Cunningham et al. (2024)</a>; <a class="cite" href="#ref-mi-bricken2023">Bricken et al. (2023)</a>; <a class="cite" href="#ref-mi-gao2024">Gao et al. (2025)</a>; <a class="cite" href="#ref-mi-templeton2024">Templeton et al. (2024)</a>; <a class="cite" href="#ref-mi-sae-vlm">Pach et al. (2025)</a>. These tools were built and tested mainly on large language models. Designing effective visual interventions therefore remains a pressing open problem. This subsection makes the case for UAEs in three steps. First, $\ell_p$-bounded perturbations are poorly suited as interpretability probes. Second, UAEs avoid this limitation by construction. Third, the two central objects of MI analysis are features and circuits, and these are exactly what UAE-based probes would illuminate.

<strong>$\ell_p$-bounded perturbations are not good probes.</strong> Traditional adversarial perturbations carry no semantic content. Their existence does convey coarse information. It shows that classifiers rely on brittle, non-semantic input directions. Robustly trained models are known to learn visibly different representations <a class="cite" href="#ref-mi-tsipras2019">Tsipras et al. (2019)</a>. But an $\ell_p$ perturbation cannot localize which human-interpretable property is involved. It is not aligned with any nameable dimension of the input. Adding imperceptible high-frequency noise to pixels reveals nothing about how a network represents color or shape. UAEs, by contrast, change specific human-interpretable properties. These are color (Section <a href="#section-3-1-1">3.1.1</a>), lighting (Section <a href="#section-3-1-2">3.1.2</a>), spatial geometry (Section <a href="#section-3-1-4">3.1.4</a>), style and semantic content (Section <a href="#section-3-4">3.4</a>). Each UAE category fixes in advance which nameable input property is manipulated. This does not make the intervention surgical. These methods were designed as attacks rather than probes. Manipulating one property typically perturbs others as a side effect. But it provides what an $\ell_p$ perturbation cannot. It provides a known, controllable intervened dimension. We take it up in Section <a href="#section-8-3">8.3</a>.

<strong>Features and superposition.</strong> <a class="cite" href="#ref-mi-superposition2022">Elhage et al. (2022)</a> formalized the superposition hypothesis, which had been observed informally in the vision circuits work (<a class="cite" href="#ref-mi-circuits-olah2020">Olah et al., 2020</a>): a network can represent more features than it has neurons by encoding them as nearly orthogonal directions in activation space, the high-dimensional vector space formed by a layer’s neuron outputs. SAEs have since been used to disentangle these superposed representations into more monosemantic features, many of which correspond to a single human-recognizable concept <a class="cite" href="#ref-mi-cunningham2024">Cunningham et al. (2024)</a>; <a class="cite" href="#ref-mi-bricken2023">Bricken et al. (2023)</a>; <a class="cite" href="#ref-mi-gao2024">Gao et al. (2025)</a>. What features vision models actually learn, and how those features relate to human-interpretable visual properties, remains an open question. UAEs offer a principled way to probe it. Suppose a color manipulation UAE disrupts a specific subset of SAE features while leaving the rest intact. This constitutes direct evidence about which learned features encode color information. It also shows whether color is cleanly separated from shape and texture in the model’s internal representation or entangled with them.

<strong>Circuits.</strong> MI has further shown that networks break down into circuits. These are subgraphs of the computational graph that implement specific functions <a class="cite" href="#ref-mi-circuits-olah2020">Olah et al. (2020)</a>; <a class="cite" href="#ref-mi-automated-circuit-discovery2023">Conmy et al. (2023)</a>; <a class="cite" href="#ref-mi-ioi-circuit2022">Wang et al. (2023b)</a>. Circuit discovery in vision models has found curve detectors, edge detectors, and object-part detectors <a class="cite" href="#ref-mi-circuits-olah2020">Olah et al. (2020)</a>; <a class="cite" href="#ref-mi-circuits-thread2020">Cammarata et al. (2020)</a>. UAEs offer a systematic way to test the functional roles of these circuits. A targeted UAE disrupts one visual property and causes misclassification. Activation patching can then localize the affected circuits. This yields causal evidence about circuit function.

<h3 id="section-8-2">8.2 Existing Evidence: Adversarial Vulnerability and Superposition</h3>

A small but growing set of studies already ties adversarial vulnerability to the representational structure that MI studies. We review this evidence along a natural progression: from what semantically structured attacks reveal as interpretability probes, to how superposition helps explain adversarial vulnerability in the first place, and finally to an open tension in how robustness and superposition relate, which marks the current limit of this understanding.

<strong>Feature-level adversaries as diagnostic probes.</strong> The clearest empirical evidence comes from <a class="cite" href="#ref-casper2022robust">Casper et al. (2022)</a>. Rather than perturbing pixels, their method searches the latent space of a generative image model. This method belongs to the UAE type in Section <a href="#section-3-5-2">3.5.2</a>. The resulting attacks therefore align with recognizable visual features that a human can read off directly. This legibility lets them expose spurious feature–class links a model has come to depend on. Crucially, the authors recast each attack as a falsifiable claim about the model’s internal representation. They tested it through copy/paste experiments at ImageNet scale. They dropped the implicated feature into unrelated images and confirmed the predicted misclassifications. The lesson is clear. Adversarial inputs with explicit semantic structure reveal properties that pixel-level perturbations leave inaccessible.

<strong>Superposition shapes vulnerability.</strong> The superposition hypothesis makes a specific explanation about why adversarial examples exist. If features are compressed into overlapping representations, a small perturbation can disrupt one feature while inadvertently activating others that share the same neural substrate <a class="cite" href="#ref-mi-superposition2022">Elhage et al. (2022)</a>. Two recent works provide early evidence for this hypothesis. <a class="cite" href="#ref-gorton2025superposition">Gorton &amp; Lewis (2025)</a> offer causal evidence, though so far at limited scale. Encouraging sparser feature use reduces superposition and improves robustness in toy models. Adversarial training reduces superposition in ResNet18. <a class="cite" href="#ref-stevinson2026adversarial">Stevinson et al. (2026)</a> supply a mechanistic account. Adversarial perturbations systematically exploit interference. This is the cross-talk that arises between features sharing the same neurons under superposition. The same feature geometry also governs attack transferability across models.

<strong>An unresolved complication.</strong> <a class="cite" href="#ref-bereska2025lossy">Bereska et al. (2025)</a> introduce an SAE-based metric for quantifying superposition. They find that adversarial training can increase the number of effective features a model represents while improving its robustness. In other words, adversarial training increases superposition. This conflicts with the finding in <a class="cite" href="#ref-gorton2025superposition">Gorton &amp; Lewis (2025)</a>, where they claim adversarial training reduces superposition. However, whether this contradicts depends on how superposition is measured: <a class="cite" href="#ref-bereska2025lossy">Bereska et al. (2025)</a> measures superposition as the ratio of effective features (the exponential of Shannon entropy over SAE activation magnitudes) to the number of neurons in the layer, whereas <a class="cite" href="#ref-gorton2025superposition">Gorton &amp; Lewis (2025)</a> use the SAE reconstruction loss as a proxy for superposition. If representation width is held fixed, more effective features does imply more superposition and the two results conflict directly; if the measures differ in normalization, they may not be comparable. Either way, the relationship between adversarial robustness and superposition is not yet settled, and the disagreement itself is a reason the connection deserves systematic study.

<h3 id="section-8-3">8.3 An Outlook: UAE Categories as Candidate Interpretability Probes</h3>

The evidence above shows the connection between adversarial examples and the study of mechanistic interpretability. In this subsection we provide an outlook of how UAEs can serve as tools for mechanistic interpretability research. Because the UAE taxonomy fixes the perturbed dimension in advance, it could turn adversarial examples from an object of study into a controllable instrument for probing vision models. We frame this as an outlook rather than a concrete proposal. We weigh both the opportunities it opens and the obstacles that stand in the way.

<strong>From emergent to specified perturbations.</strong> The feature-level attacks of <a class="cite" href="#ref-casper2022robust">Casper et al. (2022)</a> operate in an unstructured generator latent. Which visual feature an attack exploits emerges from this search rather than being chosen beforehand. It must therefore be read off each resulting image. The UAE taxonomy of Section <a href="#section-3">3</a> would remove this step. It fixes the perturbed dimension in advance. This dimension is color, lighting, geometry, texture, style, or semantic content. The resulting pattern of SAE feature disruption or circuit activation could then be traced to a known cause rather than inferred from an attack whose target was not specified.

<strong>Which features each attack disrupts could map out superposition.</strong> If the superposition account of Section <a href="#section-8-2">8.2</a> is correct, different UAE categories should produce different patterns of feature disruption. Color UAEs should disrupt color-related features and their superposed neighbors. Geometric UAEs should disrupt spatial features and theirs. Comparing disruption profiles across categories could therefore turn the taxonomy into a discriminative probe of superposition. Overlapping profiles across categories would point to feature entanglement; disjoint profiles would suggest monosemantic encoding.

<strong>A gap the emerging toolchain can now address.</strong> Vision MI lags behind its language counterpart. The tools needed to close this gap are now becoming available. SAEs have revealed interpretable features in vision models from InceptionV1 <a class="cite" href="#ref-gorton2024curve">Gorton (2024)</a> to CLIP <a class="cite" href="#ref-rao2024discover">Rao et al. (2024)</a>; <a class="cite" href="#ref-lim2025patchsae">Lim et al. (2025)</a>, and open toolkits now provide pretrained vision SAEs and circuit-analysis utilities <a class="cite" href="#ref-joseph2025prisma">Joseph et al. (2025)</a>. SAEs are also being adapted to vision-language-action models <a class="cite" href="#ref-mi-sae-vla-2026">Swann et al. (2026)</a>, and activation patching is being extended to large vision-language models <a class="cite" href="#ref-mi-che2026counting">Che et al. (2026)</a>. UAEs fit this gap naturally. They operate entirely in the image domain and can be designed to target specific representational dimensions.

<strong>Obstacles.</strong> Several difficulties will shape how far this perspective can be carried. First, the selectivity that motivates UAE-based probing is an idealization. UAE categories are not perfectly orthogonal. A style transfer attack also alters color statistics. Methods within one category differ in granularity, from global hue shifts <a class="cite" href="#ref-color-first-one-cvprw-2018">Hosseini &amp; Poovendran (2018)</a> to semantically segmented recoloring <a class="cite" href="#ref-color-natural-colorfool-nips-2022">Yuan et al. (2022)</a>. Experimental designs must control for this within-category variation. Second, the SAEs on which feature-level analysis relies exhibit known pathologies. These include feature splitting and absorption, in which one concept fragments across several learned features or hides inside another <a class="cite" href="#ref-mi-chanin2025">Chanin et al. (2025)</a>. They also include sensitivity to the training data distribution, where out-of-distribution training data induces spurious features <a class="cite" href="#ref-mi-faithfulsae2025">Cho et al. (2025)</a>. SAEs trained on clean activations may therefore not capture the features most relevant under adversarial inputs. This argues for training SAEs on the relevant input distribution. Finally, generative model-based UAEs introduce a confound of their own. The perturbation is produced by another network, so observed effects cannot always be attributed to the classifier alone. Transformation-based UAEs avoid this confound. They are the natural starting point for the agenda.

<h2 id="section-9">9 Conclusion</h2>

We surveyed unrestricted adversarial examples in image classification, a class of attacks that has developed under several different names and that no prior survey has treated as a single object of study. Our criterion throughout was that the change preserves the true label under human judgment, stays plausible to a human observer, and is not be expressible as an $\ell_p$-bounded change in pixel space. Applied to a decade of work, it yields a field that organizes into five generation mechanisms (Section <a href="#section-3">3</a>). Organizing by mechanism rather than by attacker knowledge shows what the conventional axis hides: the volume of UAE research has been stable since 2020 while its composition has turned over almost completely, from parametric transformations to diffusion-based synthesis. UAE capability now advances with generative modeling rather than independently of it.

Two gaps run through the methods, the defenses, and the evaluation practice we reviewed. Defenses usually generalize only as far as the constraint space they are built around, and no constraint space yet proposed is as broad as the perturbation space it would have to cover (Section <a href="#section-4">4</a>). Evaluation has no common scale, because the categories share no perturbation budget, so results are reported under protocols that do not compose (Section <a href="#section-6">6</a>). We therefore offer FAIR as the dimensions such a protocol would need to report rather than as a benchmark, since fixing what must be measured is a smaller claim than fixing how to score it, and it is the part the current literature can support.

The direction we consider most underexplored is the one developed in Section <a href="#section-8">8</a>. Because a UAE fixes in advance which named visual property it alters, it supplies what an $\ell_p$ perturbation cannot: an input-space intervention with a known cause that demonstrably changes the model’s decision. We offer this as a perspective rather than a result, since the evidence remains early. What a UAE finally shows is not only that a classifier can be fooled, but that it can be fooled by a change a human can name, and that name is where the question of what the model has learned becomes answerable.
